                                            The power of unentanglement without destructive interference
                                                                                   Yupan Liu∗1 and Pei Wu†2
                                             1
                                               School of Computer and Communication Sciences, École Polytechnique Fédérale de
                                                                                Lausanne
                                            2
                                              Department of Computer Science and Engineering, The Pennsylvania State University




                                                                                              Abstract
                                                        Stoquasticity, originating in physical systems free of the so-called sign problem and avoid-
                                                    ing destructive interference from sign cancellations, gives rise to StoqMA, introduced by
                                                    Bravyi, Bessen, and Terhal (2006), a quantum-inspired intermediate class between MA and
arXiv:2604.27886v2 [quant-ph] 12 Jul 2026




                                                    AM. Unentanglement similarly gives rise to QMA(2), introduced by Kobayashi, Matsumoto,
                                                    and Yamakami (CJTCS 2009), which generalizes QMA to two unentangled proofs and still
                                                    has only the trivial NEXP upper bound.
                                                        In this work, we initiate a systematic study of the power of unentanglement without
                                                    destructive interference via StoqMA(2), the class of unentangled stoquastic Merlin–Arthur
                                                    proof systems. Beyond its complexity-theoretic interest, StoqMA(2) is closely connected to
                                                    the optimality of non-negative tensor optimization algorithms. We highlight:
                                                       1. NP ⊆ StoqMA(2) with O(   e √n)-qubit proofs and completeness error 2−polylog(n) , paral-
                                                          leling the best known QMA(2) lower bounds except for perfect completeness. Mean-
                                                          while, the Sum-of-Squares algorithm of Barak, Kelner, and Steurer (STOC 2014) gives
                                                          an exponential-time upper bound for StoqMA(2). Our tightened analysis shows the
                                                          essential optimality of the parameters in both our protocol and the BKS algorithm
                                                          under the Exponential-Time Hypothesis (ETH).
                                                      2. Under nearly perfect completeness, StoqMA(2) admits sharper deterministic upper
                                                         bounds: for StoqMA(2)1 , the parameter dependence in the general ETH-optimal time
                                                         bound can be exponentially improved, or the bound achieved simultaneously with
                                                         polynomial space.
                                                      3. For logarithmic-size proofs, NP ⊆ StoqMA(2)log with completeness error O(n−2 ) and
                                                         implicitly vanishing gap. Meanwhile, StoqMA(2)log ⊆ MA, and consequently quantum-
                                                         inspired randomness enables exponentially shorter unentangled proofs even under the
                                                         standard derandomization assumption MA = NP.
                                                      4. PreciseStoqMA(2), a variant of StoqMA(2) with exponentially small promise gap, can-
                                                         not achieve perfect completeness unless EXP = NEXP. In contrast, PreciseStoqMA
                                                         achieves perfect completeness, since PSPACE ⊆ PreciseStoqMA1 .
                                                        Our lower bounds are obtained by stoquastizing the short-proof QMA(2) protocols using
                                                    distribution testing techniques. Our upper bounds for the nearly perfect completeness case
                                                    are proved via our rectangular closure testing framework, a new combinatorial technique
                                                    tailored to StoqMA(2)1 .

1       Introduction
The notion of stoquasticity, a blend of stochastic and quantum, was first introduced in [BDOT08]
to describe Hamiltonians whose off-diagonal matrix elements are real and non-positive, a con-
dition that naturally connects the model to stochastic processes such as Markov chains and
random walks. Motivated by this connection, Bravyi, Bessen, and Terhal [BBT06] introduced
the complexity class StoqMA, for which the stoquastic local Hamiltonian problem is complete.
    A useful way to understand StoqMA is to compare it directly with MA [Bab85], the class of
Merlin–Arthur proof systems and the standard randomized generalization of NP. Indeed, MA
admits a natural “quantum” formulation [BDOT08]: the verifier uses classical reversible circuits
built from Toffoli, CNOT, and X gates, together with the usual |0⟩ ancillary qubits and |+⟩
ancillary qubits that provide randomness, and then measures a designated output qubit in the
computational basis. Allowing the witness to be quantum does not increase the power of this
model, as quantum witnesses offer no advantage over classical ones.
    By contrast, in StoqMA the only formal difference is that the designated output qubit is
measured in the Hadamard basis instead. Consequently, StoqMA naturally contains MA.1 This
apparently minor syntactic change has important structural consequences. By the Perron–
Frobenius theorem in linear algebra, the acceptance probability of a StoqMA verifier is always
maximized by a witness state whose amplitudes are non-negative in the computational basis,2
and this structural property makes the underlying optimization substantially easier than in
QMA, the fully quantum analogue of NP.
    Whereas the local Hamiltonian problem, which asks one to approximate the minimum eigen-
value of an underlying Hermitian matrix, is complete for QMA, and QMA is thus at least con-
tained in PSPACE [Kit99, KSV02], the stoquastic local Hamiltonian problem can be reformulated
as the problem of estimating the trace of a power of an entrywise non-negative matrix with effi-
ciently computable local contributions. Using the techniques in [BGM06, GS89], this places Sto-
qMA at least inside AM [BBT06], and hence inside the polynomial-time hierarchy (PH) [Bab85].
In this sense, StoqMA is a semi-quantum class: it is quantum enough to go beyond MA, yet still
structured enough to admit unexpectedly classical upper bounds, unlike BQP, for which even
proving containment in PH is technically difficult in light of oracle separations [RT22].

Further perspectives on StoqMA. Beyond serving as a natural quantum-inspired interme-
diate class between MA and AM, StoqMA admits several further perspectives:
Physical perspective Traditionally, StoqMA corresponds to Hamiltonians without the sign prob-
lem: a regime with no destructive interference from sign cancellations, where random-walk-based
Monte Carlo simulations become applicable. A standard example is stoquastic k-SAT, which
is both complete for the perfect-completeness version of StoqMA (StoqMA1 ) and contained in
MA [BBT06, BT10]. More recently, this containment was extended slightly to StoqMA with
negligible completeness error [AGL25].3 Beyond being a formal model, StoqMA also captures
physically meaningful Hamiltonians, such as the transverse-field Ising model whose associated
Hamiltonian problem is StoqMA-complete [BH17] and is closely related to adiabatic quantum
computation [AL18].
Complexity-theoretic perspective. From a more modern viewpoint, StoqMA occupies a distin-
    1
      As in [BBT06, Appendix A.4], let the MA verifier be in the state |0⟩|R0 ⟩ + |1⟩|R1 ⟩ before its final mea-
surement. After appending |+⟩|0⟩ and applying√ one Toffoli gate, the resulting StoqMA verifier has the state
(|000⟩|R0 ⟩ + |100⟩|R0 ⟩ + |001⟩|R1 ⟩ + |111⟩|R1 ⟩)/ 2 before the final measurement. Hence, the acceptance proba-
bility p becomes (1 + p)/2: the accepting branch interferes constructively, while the rejecting branch is split into
orthogonal labels.
    2
      In this work, we refer to such states as non-negative states.
    3
      This improvement is achieved via a careful analysis of the underlying random walk for yes instances in [AG19,
Section 5], together with a probabilistic argument for the lazy random walk, such as [ST13, Proposition 2.5].



                                                         1
guished place in the classification of local Hamiltonian problems. In the qubit 2-local setting,
every problem falls into one of four categories: in P, NP-complete, StoqMA-complete, or QMA-
complete [CM16], yielding a quantum analogue of Schaefer’s dichotomy theorem [Sch78].4 Under
derandomization assumptions implying AM = NP [KvM02, MV05], the intermediate StoqMA-
complete level would disappear. Likewise, under the standard derandomization assumption that
MA = NP, proving StoqMA = MA would eliminate the same level.
Distribution-testing perspective. More recently, an intrinsic connection between distribution
testing and the final Hadamard-basis measurement in a StoqMA verifier has been identified
in [Liu21]. Roughly speaking, the verifier’s final measurement computes a Hellinger-affinity-
type quantity between two induced distributions that are determined jointly by the verification
circuit and the witness. This perspective also yields another non-trivial subclass eStoqMA of
StoqMA that is contained in MA via distribution-testing techniques [CR14], as well as parallel
repetition for StoqMA [Liu21], which immediately implies error reduction for StoqMA with
negligible completeness error.

Unentangled Merlin–Arthur proof systems and their motivations. Parallel to the
development of stoquastic Merlin-Arthur proof systems, the notion of unentanglement gives rise
to the class QMA(2) introduced by Kobayashi, Matsumoto, and Yamakami [KMY09], in which
the verifier has full quantum power but receives two unentangled quantum proofs, or equivalently
the witness is required to be a separable state across two registers.5 This viewpoint was then
substantially clarified by Harrow and Montanaro [HM13], who showed that QMA(k ), for up
to polynomially many provers, collapses to QMA(2). Their work established error reduction
for QMA(2), showing the class is robust under the usual choice of constant completeness and
soundness parameters.
    On the upper-bound side, however, QMA(2) is notoriously difficult. The underlying opti-
mization is over separable states and is therefore more subtle than that over arbitrary quantum
states, which obstructs many of the techniques available for QMA. For the general class, the triv-
ial upper bound of NEXP remains the best known. Nevertheless, several restricted settings are
better understood. For example, when the verifier’s induced measurements are restricted to Bell
or one-way LOCC, the corresponding subclasses are contained in QMA [BCY11, BH13, LS15].
Several further perspectives on QMA(2) are as follows:
Power of short unentangled proofs. QMA(k ) with short proofs already captures NP in strik-
ing ways. Blier and Tapp [BT12] first established that NP admits two O(log n)-qubit quantum
proofs, with a vanishing gap. A scaled-up version of this idea later implied that PreciseQMA(2) =
NEXP [Per12], and the soundness analysis was subsequently improved in [Bei10, CF13, LNN12].
Later work focused on obtaining a constant gap, a goal first realized in [ABD+ 09], and later
refined to use two O(e √n)-qubit quantum proofs by combining PCP machinery with prover-
elimination techniques from [HM13]. This line of work also yields conditional hardness of
QMA(2) under the Exponential-Time Hypothesis (ETH). Moreover, the measurement under-
lying this QMA(2) protocol can be relaxed further, even to Bell measurements [CD10], with
subsequent improvements in [CF13, NZ23]. More recently, imposing a non-negative promise
on both yes and no instances of QMA(2), thereby defining QMA+ (2), further strengthens the
power of unentanglement, yielding QMA+ (2) = NEXP [JW23], with subsequent developments
in [JW24, BFM24, BFL+ 24].
Optimization over separable states. A central way to understand QMA(2) is as tensor opti-
   4
     Other examples include local Hamiltonian problems with symmetric 2-local interactions and no 1-local terms,
which are either QMA-complete or in StoqMA [PM17], as well as a recent classification of EPR Hamiltoni-
ans [MS26], whose levels are QMA-complete, StoqMA-complete, NP-complete, and reducible to the EPR∗ problem
conjectured to be in BPP.
   5
     See also [JLW26] for a comprehensive survey about QMA(2).


                                                       2
mization in disguise: the verifier’s maximum acceptance probability is obtained by optimiz-
ing over the separable states, rather than by estimating a top eigenvalue. This connects
QMA(2) to a broader landscape of tensor optimization and hardness of approximation, includ-
ing themes around the Unique Games Conjecture [BBH+ 12]. This viewpoint has led to sev-
eral distinct algorithmic approaches. One sophisticated line of developments uses semidefinite-
programming hierarchies based on relaxations of separable states, such as PPT and extendibil-
ity [DPS04, NOP09, HNW17, FF21]. The convergence rate of the corresponding SDP hierarchies
is often guaranteed by quantum information-theoretic or de Finetti type arguments asserting that
symmetric states must be close to separable. In restricted measurement regimes such as one-way
LOCC, this SDP hierarchy yields quasipolynomial-time algorithms [BCY11, BH13, LS15]. An-
other line of attacks includes smarter search algorithms exploring the set of quantum states such
as epsilon-net methods [SW15, BH15], and Sum-of-Squares-based algorithms [BKS14, BKS17].
More recently, algebraic-geometric based approaches have been explored [HNW17, KR26]. Al-
together, this optimization perspective places QMA(2) at the interface of separability testing,
convex optimization, and classical hardness of approximation.
Physical and information-theoretic perspective. One canonical QMA(2)-complete problem is
Separable Sparse Hamiltonian [CS12]. By contrast, Separable Local Hamiltonian,
though once conjectured to be QMA(2)-complete, is in fact in QMA, and the hardness techniques
of [CS12] apply only in the sparse setting. Another natural QMA(2)-complete problem is Pure
Product Isometry Output (PIPO), which asks whether a given isometry U maps some
pure state |ψ⟩ to a state close to a pure product state [GHMW15]. Interestingly, PIPO also
serves as a starting point for proving that Low Entropy Low Energy State, namely the
intersection of the local Hamiltonian problem and the task of deciding whether a state has low
bipartite entropy, is QMA(2)-hard via a new channel-to-Hamiltonian simulation that yields a
local Hamiltonian [GK25].

From stoquastic to unentangled stoquastic. Since stoquasticity originates from sign-
problem-free physical systems, which avoid destructive interference from sign cancellations, our
model StoqMA(2) naturally combines the two themes above and captures the power of unentan-
glement without destructive interference. Algebraically, this absence of destructive interference
is reflected by non-negativity. This interaction leads to the following two questions:
      (a) Can unentanglement remain computationally powerful even without destructive
          interference? More concretely, can StoqMA(2) recover some known QMA(2)-
          type lower bounds?
    Prior work suggests that the ability to perform distribution-testing-type tasks makes StoqMA
appear more powerful than MA, with this extra power closely tied to non-negativity [Liu21];
moreover, non-negativity can dramatically strengthen unentanglement [JW23]. Together, these
results support the intuition behind Question (a).

      (b) Can stoquasticity impose new computational limitations on unentangled proof
          systems? More precisely, does StoqMA(2) admit (algorithmic) upper bounds
          beyond NEXP, still out of reach for general QMA(2)?

   Question (b) reflects the viewpoint that the intrinsic non-negativity of StoqMA makes its
convex structure easier to exploit and therefore imposes extra structure, as supported by the best
known upper bounds: QMA ⊆ SBQP [Vya03, MW05, Kup15], whereas StoqMA ⊆ SBP [BBT06],
where SBQP and SBP are variants of BQP and BPP, respectively, with a completeness-to-
soundness ratio at least 2. In the general polynomial optimization literature, a similar sign-free
phenomenon has been exploited [BGG+ 17], admitting faster approximation algorithms.
   We provide surprisingly sharp answers to these questions.

                                                3
1.1    Main results
We now present and explain our main results from both the proof-system and algorithmic per-
spectives. These two perspectives are closely connected and together establish ETH-based op-
timality for both short stoquastic unentangled proofs for NP, mirroring [ABD+ 09, CF13], and
the Barak–Kelner–Steurer algorithm [BKS14] for non-negative tensor optimization problems.

The proof-system perspective. We begin by stating our first main result concerning Sto-
qMA(2) with short proofs, given in Theorem 1.1. For convenience, we write StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K
for the class of StoqMA(k ) proof systems in which each proof has length ℓ(n), completeness
c(n), and promise gap ∆(n) := c(n) − s(n), where s(n) denotes the soundness parameter. For
simplicity, we may omit the subscript ℓ when ℓ(n) is polynomially bounded.
Theorem 1.1 (The power of StoqMA(2) with short proofs, informal version of Theorems 5.4
and 5.5). The following inclusions hold:6
                         r                            z
                                   − polylog(n)
 (1) NP ⊆ StoqMAO(  e √n) 2, 1 − 2              , Ω(1) ⊆ MA.

  (2) NP ⊆ StoqMAO(log n) 2, 1 − O n−2 , Ω n−2 ⊆ MA.
                         q                   y

    Surprisingly, Theorem 1.1(1) matches the prior constant-gap QMA(2) protocols with
sublinear-size proofs [ABD+ 09, CD10], apart from the loss of perfect completeness. A similar
short-proof phenomenon is unlikely to hold even for QMA, since otherwise it would violate
the Quantum Exponential-Time Hypothesis [CCZZ22]. Likewise, this phenomenon appears
unlikely for MA(2), which trivially collapses to MA and would therefore violate randomized ETH
(rETH) [DHM+ 14]. Therefore, since StoqMA naturally contains MA, this argument suggests
that collapsing StoqMA(2) to StoqMA, referred to as a multi-to-single witness reduction, would
incur at least a quadratic blow-up in the proof length. It turns out that this quadratic loss in
the multi-to-single witness reduction (e.g., [GJ26]) is unavoidable, assuming rETH.7
    Similarly, Theorem 1.1(2) matches the prior inverse-polynomial-gap QMA(2) protocols with
logarithmic-size proofs [BT12, Bei10, CF13, LNN12], except for the loss of perfect completeness.
Together with a sufficiently structured PCP for NEXP, such as [JW23, Theorem 7.4], we obtain
the following scaling-up version of Theorem 1.1(2):
Corollary 1.2. NEXP ⊆ StoqMA 2, 1 − 2− poly(n) , Ω 2− poly(n) ⊆ PreciseStoqMA(2).
                                   q                            y

   Here, “precise” means that the promise gap of the class variant is exponentially small. As a
consequence of Corollary 1.2 and the equivalence NEXP = PreciseQMA(2) established in [Per12],
we obtain the following equivalence:
Corollary 1.3. PreciseStoqMA(2) = NEXP = PreciseQMA(2).
    As we will discuss later in the algorithmic perspective, the protocol underlying Theo-
rem 1.1(1) is optimal under the Exponential-Time Hypothesis (ETH), when combined with
the Sum-of-Squares algorithm of [BKS14] for non-negative tensor optimization problems. In-
terestingly, our results (Theorems 1.1 and 1.8), achieving ETH-based optimality, use the class
StoqMA(2) in regimes that collapse to MA. Here, the MA upper bound in Theorem 1.1(1) is
obtained by combining [GJ26, AGL25], while the one in Theorem 1.1(2) follows from our new
upper bound for StoqMA(k )log , where the subscript log denotes logarithmic-size proofs:8

Appendix C.3), where ℓ(n) = n and n denotes the number of variables in the SAT formula. Consequently,
applying a multi-to-single witness reduction to Theorem 1.1(1) without incurring a quadratic blow-up would
refute rETH.

Theorem 1.4 (Upper bounds for StoqMA(k ) with logarithmic-size proofs, informal version

                            StoqMA(k )log ⊆ MA         while    StoqMAlog = BPP.

    Since the proof-length advantages in stoquastic short unentangled proofs remain even as-
suming the standard derandomization assumption MA = NP, our first result and its corollaries
give a positive answer to Question (a):

             Even assuming MA = NP, quantum-inspired randomness enables sublinear
                unentangled proofs and (at least) quadratic proof-length advantages.


The algorithmic perspective. Next, we turn to our second main result:

Theorem 1.5 (Upper bounds for StoqMA(k ) with perfect completeness, informal version of The-
orems 7.1 and 7.2). For all integer-valued functions ℓ(n), k(n), m0 (n), and m+ (n), and every
∆(n) ∈ (0, 1/2], where m0 (n) and m+ (n) denote the numbers of |0⟩ and |+⟩ ancillary qubits in
the underlying stoquastic verification circuit, the following inclusion holds:9
                                        kℓ                    kℓ                    
     StoqMAℓ Jk, 1, ∆K ⊆ DTISP exp O     e       max{kℓ, m+ } , O        max{kℓ, m0 , m+ }
                                            ∆2                        ∆2
                                        h                       i
                            ∩ DTIME exp O(max{kℓ,
                                               e           m+ }) .

   Here, DTISP[t, s] denotes the class of problems decidable simultaneously in deterministic
time t and space s. Taken together, Corollary 1.3 and the deterministic time bound in Theo-
rem 1.5 imply the following separation under the standard assumption EXP ̸= NEXP:

Corollary 1.6. PreciseStoqMA(2)1 ⊊ PreciseStoqMA(2) unless EXP = NEXP.

    Here, the subscript 1 indicates perfect completeness. Notably, the failure of perfect-
completeness closure for PreciseStoqMA(2), as provided in Corollary 1.6, contrasts with the
closure under perfect completeness for the general quantum classes: specifically, PreciseQMA =
PreciseQMA1 [FL16, FL18] and PreciseQMA(2) = PreciseQMA(2)1 [Per12]. Nevertheless,
this failure does not extend to the single-prover precise stoquastic setting: our inclusion
PSPACE ⊆ PreciseStoqMA1 , established in Appendix A, together with [FL16, FL18], also
implies that PreciseStoqMA is closed under perfect completeness:10

Theorem 1.7. PreciseStoqMA = PreciseStoqMA1 = PSPACE = PreciseQMA.

   Furthermore, Corollaries 1.3 and 1.6 and Theorem 1.7 together reveal a subtle distinction
among precise stoquastic proof systems: although the precise stoquastic complexity classes
match their quantum counterparts in both the single-prover and two-prover settings without
perfect completeness, this strength does not persist under perfect completeness once unentan-
glement is present.

       Our third main result records a general upper bound for StoqMA(k ):11

Theorem 1.8 (Informal version of Theorem 8.2). For every integer-valued ℓ(n) and k(n), and
for all functions c(n) ∈ (1/2, 1] and ∆(n) ∈ (0, 1/2] such that c(n) − ∆(n) ≥ 1/2, it holds that
                                                           2 2 
                         StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K ⊆ DTIME exp O    e k ℓ       .
                                                                ∆2
    Theorem 1.8 follows from the Sum-of-Squares algorithm of Barak, Kelner, and Steurer [BKS14],
as stated in Theorem 8.1. Remarkably, together with Theorem 1.1(1), our refined analysis
implies that the BKS algorithm is essentially optimal under ETH: any improvement over the
stated dependence, up to poly-logarithmic factors, on the tensor order t, corresponding to the
number of provers k, or on the dimension d = 2ℓ , corresponding to the witness length, would
yield an algorithm for SAT that runs faster than ETH permits. This ETH-based optimality also
explains an interesting subtlety concerning the loss of perfect completeness in Theorem 1.1: it
occurs precisely in the regime in which the dependence on both k and ℓ in the time complexity
in Theorem 1.5 is exponentially improved over that of the general algorithm in Theorem 1.8.
    Finally, Theorems 1.5 and 1.8 together establish non-trivial algorithmic upper bounds for
StoqMA(2). These results contrast with the trivial NEXP upper bound for QMA(2), which
remains the state of the art more than two decades after the introduction of QMA(2) [KMY09].
Consequently, our upper bounds give a positive answer to Question (b) and indicate that

            Stoquasticity imposes exploitable structure in unentangled proof systems,
                         yielding upper bounds much better than NEXP.

proved in the follow-up work [GJ26] are shown in blue shaded boxes with dashed borders.

1.2    Proof techniques
To explain our main results in Section 1.1, we first present basic properties of StoqMA(k ) and its
symmetric variant, which lead to the robustness of StoqMA(2). Equipped with these properties,
we then explain how our lower and upper bounds are achieved.

                                                6
Basic properties of StoqMA(k ). We start with a consequence of parallel repetition for Sto-
qMA(k ) (Theorem 3.3), where the subscript “1 − negl” denotes negligible completeness error:

Theorem 1.9 (Error reduction for StoqMA(k )1−negl , informal version of Theorem 3.3 and Corol-
lary 3.4). Let ε(n) be a negligible function, and let ∆(n) ≥ 1/
                                                              l poly(n) denotemthe promise gap.
                                                                           1
                                                                            
Then, for every polynomially bounded r(n) satisfying r(n) ≥ n log2 1−ε−∆       , it holds that

                       StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMArℓ k, 1 − ε′ , Ω(1) .
                                                         q              y

Here, ε′ (n) is also a negligible function.

    It is noteworthy that Theorem 1.9 mirrors parallel repetition for StoqMA [Liu21, Section 5],
but with parameter loss. The main obstacle to extending the approach of [Liu21, Section 5] to
StoqMA(2) is that its soundness analysis relies crucially on the eigenvalue properties of stoquastic
Hermitian matrices,12 whereas the analogous argument fails for separable values of stoquastic
Hermitian matrices in general;13 see Remark 3.5. Instead, we work directly with separable values
of stoquastic positive semi-definite (PSD) matrices, thereby showing that separable values are
multiplicative for stoquastic PSD matrices (Lemma 3.8). Interestingly, for general QMA(2), such
a multiplicative property holds only for separable Hermitian matrices; cf. [HM13, Lemma 10].

    However, unlike QMA(2), which admits error reduction [KMY09, ABD+ 09, HM13], general
error reduction for StoqMA remains open and would collapse StoqMA to MA [AGL25]. Nev-
ertheless, two basic properties for QMA(2), namely prover compression [HM13] and witness
symmetrization [ABD+ 09], still admit stoquastic analogues with only a quadratic gap loss:

Theorem 1.10 (Robustness of StoqMA(2), informal version of Theorems 4.1 and 4.4). Let ε(n)
be the completeness error, and let ∆(n) be the promise gap. Then the following inclusions hold:

  (1) Prover compression. For every polynomially bounded integer-valued k(n) > 2,
                  StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAkℓ 2, 1 − O(ε∆), Ω(∆2 ) .
                                                   q                    y


  (2) Witness symmetrization. For every polynomially bounded integer-valued k(n) ≥ 2,
                 StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ SymStoqMAO(ℓ log k) Jk, 1 − ε − O(∆), Ω(∆)K,                               (1.1a)
              SymStoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAℓ k, 1 − O(ε∆), Ω(∆2 ) .                                             (1.1b)
                                                q                       y


    When the completeness error is negligible, by combining Theorems 1.9 and 1.10(1), we obtain
StoqMA(k )1−negl = StoqMA(2)1−negl for every polynomially bounded k ≥ 2.14 The proof of
Theorem 1.10(1) adopts the construction underlying Theorem 1.9 and the improved analysis
of [SW22] to implement the product test (cf. [HM13, Protocol 1]). Without error reduction,
the quadratic gap loss is obtained via optimizing the branch occurrence probability in [HM13,
Protocol 2], rather than using the original value 1/2. To prove Theorem 1.10(2), the inclusion
in Equation (1.1b) is obtained similarly by refining the construction in [ABD+ 09, Lemma 4.8].
The most interesting direction is the inclusion in Equation (1.1a), which improves the witness
length from kℓ in [ABD+ 09, Lemma 4.8] to O(ℓ log k) while preserving an Ω(∆) promise gap.
    The main idea behind Equation (1.1a) is a coupon-collector-type branch argument: instead
of asking the prover to send k identical copies of the k-tuple witness state |ψ1 ⟩|ψ2 ⟩ · · · |ψk ⟩, the
                                                                                      ⊗r
verifier asks for k bundles of coherent labeled states, of the form √1k j∈[k] |j⟩|ψj ⟩ . Each bun-
                                                                       P

dle contains r = O(log k) independent labeled basis branches, so in the branch-weight analysis
  12
     For convenience, we refer to entrywise non-negative matrices as stoquastic matrices.
  13
     Roughly speaking, for any StoqMA(2) verification circuit Vx , the maximum acceptance probability of Vx

the verifier encounters many labels from [k]. By the coupon-collector intuition, a constant total
branch weight is supported on configurations in which these labels contain enough information
to select one branch labeled 1, one branch labeled 2, and so on, using distinct bundles. When
this event occurs, the verifier routes the chosen registers to the original StoqMA(k ) verifier; oth-
erwise, it applies a fixed dummy behavior. This construction preserves soundness and loses only
a constant fraction of the original promise gap.

Lower bounds via distribution testing. The connection between distribution testing and
StoqMA was originally revealed in [Liu21]. More precisely, any stoquastic verification circuit can
be viewed as a reversible branch-overlap test, implicitly in [Liu21, Theorem 22]: given a pair of
reversible circuits (R0 , R1 ) and a non-negative witness state |Ψ⟩, the corresponding stoquastic
verification circuit accepts with probability
               1 + ⟨R0 (Ψ)|R1 (Ψ)⟩
                                   , where |Rb (Ψ)⟩ := Rb |Ψ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩, ∀b ∈ {0, 1}.         (1.2)
                         2
    We extend this viewpoint to the symmetric unentangled setting SymStoqMA(k ), where the
witness state is constrained to have the form |Ψ⟩ := |ψ⟩⊗K .15 This variant is equivalent to
StoqMA(k ) up to a quadratic gap loss (Theorem 1.10(2)). Instead of using one witness to
induce one distributional object, the symmetric tensor-power witness creates many coherent
factor branches whose squared amplitudes behave as a product distribution.
    Consequently, our lower-bound constructions can be viewed as coherent analogues of dis-
tribution testing. In a classical distribution-testing algorithm, one draws independent samples
from an unknown distribution. In SymStoqMA(k ), however, no such intermediate measurement
is performed. Let G = (V, E) be the constraint graph, where V contains n vertices. Instead, the
verifier receives the witness |Ψ⟩, where a computational-basis branch is labeled by a tuple
                                       ((v1 , a1 ), . . . , (vK , aK )) ∈ (V × Σ)K .
We call (vi , ai ) the vertex-label branch in the i-th tensor factor of |Ψ⟩, and the full tuple a
K-vertex-label branch. The squared amplitude of this K-vertex-label branch is
                K
                Y                                                                      X
                      p(vi , ai ),   where p(vi , ai ) := αv2i ,ai and |ψ⟩ =                 αv,a |v, a⟩.
                i=1                                                             (v,a)∈V ×Σ

    Therefore the coherent branch weights are governed by the product distribution p⊗K . This
product-distribution interpretation is used only in the analysis: it describes the total squared
amplitude of accepting or rejecting branches, not outcomes of actual measurements. This dis-
tinction is important, since actual polynomially many classical samples would suggest an MA
containment, as in [Liu21, Section 3.1].

    We first explain Theorem 1.1(2) as a warm-up, which can be viewed as a stoquastic analogue
of [BT12, Bei10, CF13, LNN12]. Our starting point is the Gap Constraint Graph Problem
(GapCG) used in Dinur’s PCP theorem [Din07]. For a satisfying labeling ι : V → Σ, where
(ι(u), ι(v)) ∈ Ruv for each (u, v) ∈ E, the honest one-copy witness is:
                                              1 X
                                     |ϕL ⟩ = p         |v, ι(v)⟩.
                                              |V | v∈V
The verifier receives the witness state |Φ⟩ := |ϕL ⟩ ⊗ |ϕL ⟩. A cheating witness may try to
concentrate branch weight on a small region of the graph, making an unsatisfying labeling appear
satisfying. This observation motivates the following simple branch-local protocol Abranch for a
fixed 2-vertex-label branch ((u, a), (v, b)). The protocol chooses the uniformity or consistency
test with probability 1/2 each and accepts if the chosen test accepts:
 15
      Although K = k, we use K here to emphasize K identical copies of |ψ⟩, rather than the number of provers.


                                                            8
   • Uniformity test: Reject if u = v;

   • Consistency test: Reject if u = v and a ̸= b; or (u, v) ∈ E and (a, b) ∈
                                                                            / Ruv .

    Since only two vertex-label branches are present, a direct calculation shows that the relevant
collision and edge-detection probabilities are of order 1/ poly(n). To implement Abranch as a
stoquastic verification circuit, we express the two acceptance conditions corresponding to the
above tests, as predicates Aunif and Acons , implement them by classical reversible circuits Γunif
and Γcons , and combine these circuits using an additional |+⟩ ancillary qubit to obtain a reversible
circuit Γbranch for the uniform mixture of the two tests. The actual stoquastic verifier is then
obtained by applying the branch-overlap test to the circuit pair (Γbranch , I) on the input state
|+⟩|Ψ⟩|0̄⟩, where |Ψ⟩ equals |Φ⟩ for yes instances.

    We next explain Theorem 1.1(1), a stoquastic analogue of [ABD+ 09, CD10]. We use the same
starting point, GapCG from [Din07], and the same one-copy witness |ϕL ⟩. To achieve a constant
promise gap, the short-proof protocols of [CD10] use O(      e √n) identical copies of |ϕL ⟩, which can
be compressed into two O(     e √n)-qubit proofs using the prover-compression technique [HM13].
Their perfect completeness relies on (at least) Bell-type measurements, while constant soundness
follows from a birthday-paradox analysis.
    To stoquastize these protocols, the main technical challenge is that the uniformity test is
typically implemented using the quantum Fourier transform [ABD+ 09, CD10], and hence is
not stoquastic. One might instead use the two-branch uniformity test from Abranch , but this
gives only a 1/ poly(n) promise gap. To recover a constant gap, we use Paninski’s coincidence-
based uniformity-testing idea [Pan08]: in distribution testing, deviations from uniformity can
                                                                                                     √
be detected from the collision pattern among very sparse samples, with the optimal Θ( n)
behavior for constant-distance testing. Paninski’s test is particularly useful because it separates
marginal uniformity from label consistency, making the soundness analysis modular and leading
to a predicate Abunif . With a refined soundness analysis, a similar birthday-paradox mechanism
applies to coherent K-vertex-label branches. We thus obtain a branch-local protocol Abbranch for
         e √n)-vertex-label branch: the protocol accepts if both tests A

Upper bounds via rectangular closure testing. To derive upper bounds for StoqMA(2)1 , a
natural starting point is to extend the known random-walk approach used to prove StoqMA1 ⊆
MA to the unentangled setting, which relies crucially on the local optimality phenomenon of
stoquastic k-SAT. This approach [BBT06, BT10] uses this structure dynamically: perfect com-
pleteness can be interpreted as the existence of a clean connected component in the configuration
graph of a stoquastic k-SAT instance, and a random walk from a single seed string suffices to
test this component.
    However, this local structure does not directly extend to StoqMA(2). Given a pair of re-
versible circuits (R0 , R1 ), Equation (1.2) allows us equivalently to consider the circuit pair (Γ, I),
where Γ = R0† R1 . Consequently, for any 2ℓ-qubit non-negative witness state |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ |ψ2 ⟩, perfect
completeness yields the following identity, with r = m+ in the notation of Theorem 1.5:
                          Γ(|ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ |ψ2 ⟩)|0̄⟩|+⟩⊗r = (|ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ |ψ2 ⟩)|0̄⟩|+⟩⊗r .                  (1.3)
Since Γ is a bijective permutation, Equation (1.3) implies support invariance. More precisely,
if |ψ1 ⟩ and |ψ2 ⟩ have supports S and T , respectively, then the joint support is the rectangle
S × T ; moreover, one may take the corresponding product       subset state |S⟩ ⊗ |T ⟩, where the
subset state of S ⊆ {0, 1} is denoted by |S⟩ =
                            ℓ                      √ 1 P
                                                           j∈S |j⟩. This rectangular structure is
                                                          |S|
global and therefore breaks the direct local optimality analogy with StoqMA1 : a clean connected
component may be highly non-rectangular and hence may not support any product witness.

                                                    9
    Nevertheless, our combinatorial approach recovers a global analogue of local random-walk
exploration by testing a property we call rectangular closure. The key observation from Equa-
tion (1.3) is that, whenever (s, t, u) satisfies s ∈ S, t ∈ T , and u ∈ U := {0, 1}r , every transition
of Γ from (s, t, 0̄, u) to (s′ , t′ , a0 , u′ ) must satisfy a0 = 0̄ and (s′ , t′ , u′ ) ∈ S×T ×U . In other words,
a closed rectangle never sends a valid configuration outside the zero-ancilla sector. Our rectan-
gular closure testing algorithm T formalizes this idea: starting from every seed pair (s0 , t0 ), it
repeatedly closes the current rectangle under all transitions of Γ, adding every pair (s′ , t′ ) forced
by the transition relation underlying Γ. This deterministic process tests not only pairs reached
by an ordinary walk, but also all crossed pairs required by separability.
    The resulting algorithm is therefore global rather than local. For yes instances, Equation (1.3)
guarantees that a seed inside a closed rectangle never changes the zero-ancilla register. For no
instances, the soundness promise implies that every non-closed rectangle has noticeable escaping
mass; by reversibility, this escaping mass forces multiplicative growth of the rectangular closure.
If γ denotes the rejection probability for no instances, which is comparable to the promise gap
∆, then it suffices to run L = Θ(ℓ/γ) closure rounds so that every seed encounters a nonzero
ancilla transition. Implementing our L-round rectangular closure testing algorithm recursively
yields an upper bound that is simultaneously exponential-time and polynomial-space for inverse-
polynomial gaps, while an explicit-table implementation yields a singly exponential-time upper
bound for exponentially small gaps.
    Finally, by replacing exact invariance (Equation (1.3)) with an approximate version, one
can extend our upper bounds to the nearly perfect-completeness regime. Combining our upper
bounds with our prover-compression technique (Corollary 4.2) establishes Theorem 1.5.

A general upper bound via Sum-of-Squares. The upper bound of StoqMA(k ) ⊆ EXP is
a corollary of the Sum-of-Squares algorithm of Barak, Kelner, and Steurer [BKS14].
    After passing to the symmetric form of the protocol, the acceptance probability can be
written as a tensor optimization over the unit sphere
                                              max Tr M (xxT )⊗t ,
                                             ∥x∥=1

where M acting on (Cd )⊗t is the entrywise non-negative matrix induced by the stoquastic verifier,
t is the tensor order corresponding to the number of provers, and d = 2ℓ is the dimension of each
proof. The BKS algorithm relaxes this optimization q to a bounded-degree pseudoexpectation
and rounds it using the non-negative vector x∗ = E[x  e 2 ]. The role of non-negativity is crucial
                                                       i           i
here: it allows the rounded vector to be compared directly with the pseudoexpectation without
cancellations from signs or phases.
    At a high level, the analysis views the pseudoexpectation as defining a joint exchangeable
distribution on random variables A1 , . . . , At . If these variables are close to independent, then the
above direct rounding already gives a good approximation. If they are noticeably correlated, the
BKS conditioning step reweights the pseudo-distribution by a square monomial, which decreases
an entropy potential. Since the entropy is bounded by log d, only finitely many conditioning
steps are possible before rounding succeeds. BKS’s analysis yields a degree-O(t3 log d) SoS upper
bound, which corresponds to an SDP of size exp(O(t3 log2 d)).
    We slightly tighten BKS’s analysis, exploiting the symmetry in the entropy argument, avoid-
ing a wasteful triangle inequality, yielding an improved degree-O(t2 log d) SoS upper bound.
Although the improvement looks quantitatively modest, it makes the parameter dependence
tight. Combined with Theorem 1.1, the degree-O(t2 log d) SoS gives rise to a SAT algorithm
which matches the ETH barrier up to logarithmic and gap-dependent factors: an improvement
to exp((t log d)2−Ω(1) ) time of the SoS algorithm, or any improvement of our protocols Theo-
rem 1.1 with shorter proofs or smaller number of provers would yield a subexponential-time
algorithm for SAT.


                                                        10
1.3      Discussion and open problems
Our results in Theorem 1.1 stoquastize known short unentangled quantum proofs for NP, with
either constant gap [ABD+ 09, CD10, NZ23] or vanishing gap [BT12, Bei10, CF13, LNN12],
and push the upper bound down to MA, which is the best possible target under the standard
derandomization assumption MA = NP. This short-proof picture raises a natural question of
whether short unentangled quantum proofs can yield lower bounds beyond NP.
    An intriguing target is QCMA. One possible starting point is the Ground State Connec-
tivity Problem (GSCON): as established in [CNS18], GSCON ∈ QMAO(n)       e  J2, 1, 1/ poly(n)K,
which suggests the following question:

  (i) Can this protocol be made substantially shorter, or have constant promise gap (assuming
      PCP for QCMA)? More broadly, can one identify a natural QCMA-complete or QMA-
      complete problem that admits a genuinely short QMA(2) proof system?

   From the algorithmic perspective, our upper bounds leave open the possibility of better
time-space trade-off algorithms for StoqMA(2) and, more generally, for non-negative tensor op-
timization problems. The Barak–Kelner–Steurer algorithm [BKS14], with a refined analysis
in Theorem 1.8, achieves near-optimal exponential-time dependence under ETH, but its space
usage is not obviously polynomial. In parameter regimes where the ETH-based optimality bound
does not apply, Theorem 1.5 matches the dependence on the parameters k and ℓ of the upper
bound that is ETH-optimal in the applicable regimes, while using only polynomial space. By
contrast, our fastest algorithm in Theorem 1.5 achieves an exponential improvement over the
ETH-optimal bound in the parameters k and ℓ. A natural open problem is therefore:

 (ii) Is there a randomized or deterministic algorithm for StoqMA(2) with proof length ℓ and
      constant gap that runs in time exp(O(ℓ))
                                          e     and uses only poly(n, ℓ) space? More generally,
      can algorithms for non-negative tensor optimization avoid explicitly storing exponentially
      large matrices or high-degree SDP objects while preserving their time complexity?

1.4      Related work
Most prior work connecting to StoqMA concerns stoquastic Hamiltonians. A line of work start-
ing from [Bra15, Liu21] studies restricted variants of stoquastic local Hamiltonian problems,
especially under additional promises on the ground state or its approximation that lead to MA
containments. This perspective has proved useful beyond the stoquastic setting: analogous MA
containments extend to general local Hamiltonians either with constant precision [GL23, Le 25]
or when the ground state admits a succinct representation [Jia25], with improved hardness results
in [WL25].16 A common formulation of the former includes a guiding state as part of the input,
and its stoquastic counterpart was recently studied from several perspectives [Wai25, HBS26].
    Further work on stoquastic Hamiltonians has studied the distinction between global and
termwise stoquasticity [IPM+ 20], the complexity of curing the sign problem [KT19, KMP+ 20],
oracle separations between stoquastic adiabatic and classical computation [Has21, GHV21], the
equivalence of logarithmically many adaptive queries and polynomially many parallel queries to
a StoqMA oracle [GPY20], and the StoqMA-hardness of geometrically local stoquastic Hamil-
tonians [REG26, WB26]. In addition, a stoquastic version of the Consistency of Local Density
Matrices Problem was proposed in [Liu07], although its StoqMA-completeness remains open.
Very recently, a homology-related MA-complete problem was identified in [HGRD25].
    For QMA(2), alternative proofs of parallel repetition for SepQMA(2) appear in [GSU13,
LW17]. Quantum upper bounds for QMA(2) were given in [GSS+ 22, GR26a] in terms of certain
low levels of different quantum analogues of the polynomial-time hierarchy. In addition, a recent
work identifies a natural QMA(2)-complete problem related to quantum channel testing [CM25].
 16
      In addition, a related constant-precision setting with an NP containment was studied in [WFC24].


                                                       11
Finally, another recent work [BHW25] shows that QMA(2) collapses to QMA assuming a certain
non-standard version of the quantum PCP conjecture.

Concurrent and follow-up work. The concurrent work of [GR26b], which appeared two
days after ours, establishes a complete stoquastic counterpart of [CS12] with respect to spar-
sity: Stoquastic Sparse Hamiltonians Problem is StoqMA-complete, while its separable
version is StoqMA(2)-complete. That work also considers a stoquastic version of prover com-
pression [HM13, SW22], similar in spirit to Theorem 4.7 of our work. Their result obtains a
quadratic loss in the promise gap under the additional assumption that the completeness error is
negligible, whereas our corresponding result achieves the same gap loss without this assumption.
    The follow-up work of [GJ26], which was released fifteen days after our work, establishes a
multi-to-single witness reduction for stoquastic Merlin–Arthur proof systems, thereby collapsing
StoqMA(k ) to StoqMA for every polynomially bounded k ≥ 2. While our algorithmic upper-
bound results (Theorems 1.5 and 1.8) remain state of the art, the complexity-theoretic upper
bound for StoqMA(k ) was significantly improved in [GJ26]. It is important to note, however,
that multi-to-single witness reduction and prover compression are different phenomena. More
precisely, the latter is more efficient than the former with respect to the proof length ℓ:
    • Applying the multi-to-single witness reduction in [GJ26] to StoqMA(k ) with proof length
      ℓ results in StoqMA with proof length O(k 4 ℓ2 /∆3 ).17

    • Applying prover compression in Theorem 1.10(1) to the same class results in StoqMA(2)
      with proof length kℓ.
This comparison aligns with the rETH-based quadratic lower bound with respect to the proof
length ℓ for multi-to-single witness reductions, as sketched immediately after Theorem 1.1.


2     Preliminaries
We assume that the reader has basic familiarity with quantum computation and quantum in-
formation theory. For background, the textbooks [NC10] and [dW19] provide useful starting
points. We adopt the following notation throughout the paper:
    • For every positive integer n, we write [n] := {1, 2, . . . , n} and JnK := {0, 1, . . . , n − 1}.

    • The notation O(f
                   e ) stands for O(f polylog(f )).

    • The notations ln and log denote the natural logarithm and the logarithm to the base 2,
      respectively.

    • A function µ : N → R≥0 is negligible if, for every integer c ≥ 1, there exists an integer
      nc > 0 such that µ(n) < n−c for all n ≥ nc .

    • The notation poly(·) denotes an unspecified polynomial, and polylog(·) denotes an unspec-
      ified polylogarithmic factor. We also write negl for an unspecified negligible function.

    • We adopt I[·] to denote the indicator function of an event or predicate.
    In addition, we denote a promise problem by a pair I = (Iyes , Ino ) of disjoint subsets of
{0, 1}∗ . Inputs in Iyes are called yes instances, and inputs in Ino are called no instances.
Whenever convenient, we write x ∈ I to mean that x ∈ Iyes ∪ Ino . No condition is imposed on
inputs outside Iyes ∪ Ino .

Linear algebra conventions. We use |0̄⟩ and |+̄⟩ as shorthand for |0⟩⊗a and |+⟩⊗a , respec-

for the Euclidean norm of a vector |ψ⟩ in Dirac notation.
Non-negative states and matrices. A d-dimensional quantum state |ψ⟩, viewed as a unit vector,
is called non-negative if ⟨x|ψ⟩ ≥ 0 for all x ∈ [d]. For any non-negative state |ψ⟩, the support of
|ψ⟩ is denoted by supp(|ψ⟩) := {s ∈ [d] | ⟨s|ψ⟩ > 0}, which consists of the basis indices (typically
binary strings) corresponding to nonzero amplitudes. For any nonempty set S ⊆ {0, 1}n with
d = 2n , the corresponding   subset state, a notion first considered in [Wat00, Section 3], is defined
by |S⟩ := √1                In addition, a d1 × d2 matrix M is called entrywise non-negative if
                 P
                   i∈S |i⟩.
              |S|
⟨x|M |y⟩ ≥ 0 for all x ∈ [d1 ] and y ∈ [d2 ].
Matrices and matrix norms. A square matrix A is Hermitian if A = A† . A Hermitian matrix
A is positive semi-definite, written A ⪰ 0 or 0 ⪯ A, if ⟨ψ|A|ψ⟩ ≥ 0 for every vector |ψ⟩.
Moreover, we say that a positive semi-definite matrix A is a density matrix if Tr(A) = 1. For
Hermitian matrices A and B of the same dimension, we write A ⪯ B if B − A is positive semi-
definite. pIn addition, we denote the trace norm by ∥A∥1 := Tr(|A|), the Frobenius norm by
∥A∥2 := Tr(A† A), and the operator norm by ∥A∥∞ := σmax (A), where σmax (A) denotes the
largest singular value of A. For simplicity, we also write ∥A∥ = ∥A∥∞ .
State spaces. Let S(d) be the set of d-dimensional unit vectors, and let S+ (d) be the set of
d-dimensional non-negative unit vectors. Similarly, let D(d) be the set of d-dimensional density
matrices, and let D+ (d) be the set of d-dimensional entrywise non-negative density matrices.

2.1    Information-theoretic measures
We first list several useful information-theoretic measures for quantum states, together with
their basic properties.

Definition 2.1 (Trace distance). Let ρ0 and ρ1 be quantum states in D(N ). The trace distance
between ρ0 and ρ1 is defined by
                                              1
                                T(ρ0 , ρ1 ) := Tr(|ρ0 − ρ1 |).
                                              2
Definition 2.2 (Fidelity). Let ρ0 and ρ1 be quantum states in D(N ). The (Uhlmann) fidelity
and the squared fidelity between ρ0 and ρ1 are defined by
                            √ √            √      √
                                          q
          F(ρ0 , ρ1 ) := Tr| ρ0 ρ1 | = Tr    ρ0 ρ1 ρ0 and F2 (ρ0 , ρ1 ) := F(ρ0 , ρ1 )2 .

   When one of the states is pure, say ρ0 = |ψ⟩⟨ψ| and ρ1 = ρ, it is easy to verify that
                                       F2 (|ψ⟩⟨ψ|, ρ) = ⟨ψ|ρ|ψ⟩.                                (2.1)
When both states are pure, the following identity holds; see, e.g., [NC10, Section 9.2.3]:
                                                                                                (2.2)
                                                p
                     T(|ψ0 ⟩⟨ψ0 |, |ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |) = 1 − F2 (|ψ0 ⟩⟨ψ0 |, |ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |).
By the data-processing inequality for the trace distance, e.g., [NC10, Theorem 9.2], we obtain:

Lemma 2.3 (Measurement bound for trace distance). Let ρ0 and ρ1 be quantum states in D(N ).
For every two-outcome measurement {Π, I − Π} with 0 ⪯ Π ⪯ I, it holds that
                                  |Tr(Πρ0 ) − Tr(Πρ1 )| ≤ T(ρ0 , ρ1 ).


   Next, we list several information-theoretic measures for probability distributions, together
with their basic properties.


                                                  13
Definition 2.4 (Total variation distance). Let p0 and p1 be probability distributions over [N ].
The total variation distance between p0 and p1 is defined by
                                              1 X
                              TV(p0 , p1 ) :=      |p0 (x) − p1 (x)|.
                                              2
                                                               x∈[N ]

Definition 2.5 (Hellinger distance). Let p0 and p1 be probability distributions over [N ]. The
squared Hellinger distance and Hellinger distance between p0 and p1 are defined by
                                  X p                                 q
              2
             dH (p0 , p1 ) := 1 −    p0 (x)p1 (x) and dH (p0 , p1 ) := d2H (p0 , p1 ).
                                        x∈[N ]

Definition 2.6 (Kullback–Leibler divergence). Let p0 and p1 be probability distributions over
[N ]. The Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between p0 and p1 is defined by
                                            X                p0 (x)
                            KL(p0 ∥p1 ) :=        p0 (x) log        .
                                                             p1 (x)
                                                          x : p0 (x)>0

Lemma 2.7 (Adapted from [Rei89, Equation (3.3.9)]). Let p0 and p1 be probability distributions
over [N ]. Then the following inequality holds:
                                                 2d2H (p0 , p1 ) ≤ KL(p0 ∥p1 ).
Definition 2.8 (Shannon entropy). Let p be a probability distribution over [N ]. The Shannon
entropy of p is defined by               X
                             H(p) := −       p(x) log(p(x)),
                                                           x∈[N ]

with the convention that 0 log(0) = 0.
Equivalently, if X is a random variable taking values in Ω, then the law, or distribution, of X
is the probability distribution pX over Ω denoted by pX (x), and we write
                              H(X) := H(pX ),               where pX (x) := Pr[X = x].
Definition 2.9 (Conditional entropy and mutual information). For jointly distributed random
variables X and Y , the law of (X, Y ) is the joint distribution pX,Y , and we define the joint
entropy and the conditional entropy of X given Y by
                      H(X, Y ) := H(pX,Y )              and      H(X | Y ) := H(X, Y ) − H(Y ).
The mutual information between X and Y is defined by
                                       I(X; Y ) := H(X) + H(Y ) − H(X, Y ).

2.2   (Stoquastic) separable values of square Hermitian matrices
Let M be a square Hermitian matrix of dimension d1 d2 · · · dk . Let Sep(d1 , · · · , dk ) and
Sep+ (d1 , · · · , dk ) denote, Q
                                respectively, the set of separable states and the set of separable
non-negative states on D j∈[k] dj :
                                        

       Sep(d1 , · · · , dk ) := conv{|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |⊗· · ·⊗|ψk ⟩⟨ψk | | |ψj ⟩ ∈ S(dj ) for every j ∈ [k]},
      Sep+ (d1 , · · · , dk ) := conv{|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |⊗· · ·⊗|ψk ⟩⟨ψk | | |ψj ⟩ ∈ S+ (dj ) for every j ∈ [k]},
where conv(S) denotes the convex hull of a set S.
   Following [HM13, Equation (5)], we define the corresponding support functions, called the
separable value and the stoquastic separable value, by
        hSep(d1 ,··· ,dk ) (M ) :=                max                 Tr(M (|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |⊗· · ·⊗|ψk ⟩⟨ψk |)),       (2.3a)
                                     |ψ1 ⟩∈S(d1 ),··· ,|ψk ⟩∈S(dk )

      hSep+ (d1 ,··· ,dk ) (M ) :=                 max                    Tr(M (|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |⊗· · ·⊗|ψk ⟩⟨ψk |)).   (2.3b)
                                     |ψ1 ⟩∈S+ (d1 ),··· ,|ψk ⟩∈S+ (dk )



                                                                14
    By definition, it follows that hSep+ (d1 ,··· ,dk ) (M ) ≤ hSep(d1 ,··· ,dk ) (M ). When all proofs have
the same dimension, namely d1 = · · · = dk = d, we adopt the notation
             hSep[k;d] (M ) := hSep(d,··· ,d) (M ) and hSep+ [k;d] (M ) := hSep+ (d,··· ,d) (M )
for convenience. From Equation (2.3), it is straightforward to see the following identity, which
also immediately applies to hSep+ (d1 ,··· ,dk ) :

Proposition 2.10. For all real numbers a, b ≥ 0, it holds that
                          hSep(d1 ,··· ,dk ) (aM + bI) = a · hSep(d1 ,··· ,dk ) (M ) + b.

2.3    Stoquastic algorithmic toolkit
The following definition is implicitly given in [BBT06, Definition 4]:

Definition 2.11 (Stoquastic verification circuit). We say that an n-qubit quantum circuit Q is a
stoquastic verification circuit if Q acts on the registers W, A0 , and A+ , uses only Toffoli, CNOT,
and X gates before the final measurement, and then measures the designated output qubit in the
register O in the Hadamard basis. The circuit Q accepts if and only if the final measurement yields
the outcome +. The witness register W contains an mw -qubit state, and the ancillary registers
A0 and A+ are initialized to |0⟩⊗m0 and |+⟩⊗m+ , respectively, where n = mw + m0 + m+ . The
remaining qubits are contained in the register R, which has n − 1 qubits.

    The SWAP test was originally proposed for pure states in [BCWdW01] and was later shown
to extend to mixed states [KMY09]:

Lemma 2.12 (SWAP test for mixed states, adapted from [KMY09, Proposition 9]). Let ρ0
and ρ1 be two m-qubit quantum states, which may in general be mixed. Then there exists an
explicit (2m + 1)-qubit stoquastic circuit that accepts with probability (1 + Tr(ρ0 ρ1 ))/2. This
circuit implementation uses a single copy of each ρ0 and ρ1 , together with m Toffoli gates and
2m CNOT gates.18

   We also need the reversible branch-overlap test, which we refer to simply as the branch-
overlap test, as stated in Lemma 2.13. This test is implicit in [Liu21, Theorem 22], with an
explicit circuit implementation given in [Liu21, Figure 1], and was originally inspired by the
SWAP test [BCWdW01].

Lemma 2.13 (Reversible branch-overlap test, adapted from [Liu21, Theorem 22]). Let R0 and
R1 be polynomial-size classical reversible circuits consisting only of Toffoli, CNOT, and X gates.
Assume that both R0 and R1 act on the same m(n)-qubit full input state
                                     |Ψfull ⟩ := |Ψ⟩ ⊗ |0⟩⊗m0 ⊗ |+⟩⊗r ,
where |Ψ⟩ is the non-negative input state, and m0 (n) and r(n) are polynomially bounded integer-
valued functions. For b ∈ {0, 1}, define |Rb (Ψ)⟩ := Rb |Ψfull ⟩. Then there exists an explicit
(m + 1)-qubit stoquastic circuit that accepts with probability (1 + ⟨R0 (Ψ)|R1 (Ψ)⟩)/2. This circuit
implementation uses a single query to each of R0 and R1 , together with one additional |+⟩
ancillary qubit and a single X gate.


3     Parallel repetition in stoquastic Merlin–Arthur proof systems
We begin by defining StoqMA(k ) via stoquastic verification circuits (Definition 2.11), which
naturally extends [BBT06, Definition 4] to the setting of unentangled witness states:
  18
     A Fredkin gate, namely, a controlled-swap gate on two qubits, can be implemented exactly using two CNOT
gates and a Toffoli gate; see, e.g., [NC10, Exercise 4.25].


                                                        15
Definition 3.1 (StoqMA(k )). A promise problem I = (Iyes , Ino ) is in StoqMAℓ (k, c, s) if there
exists a stoquastic verifier VI such that for every input x ∈ I, the verifier is specified by an
n-qubit stoquastic verification circuit VI (x) that acts on the kℓ-qubit witness register W together
with the ancillary registers A0 and A+ , which are initialized to |0⟩⊗m0 and |+⟩⊗m+ , respectively,
where n = kℓ + m0 + m+ , and produces a Hadamard-basis measurement outcome on the single-
qubit register O together with the remaining qubits in the register R, such that the following hold
for efficiently computable functions c(n), s(n), and ℓ(n):

Completeness. If x ∈ Iyes , then there exists a collection of unentangled states |ψ1 ⟩, · · · , |ψk ⟩,
   each on at most ℓ(n) qubits and with non-negative amplitudes such that
                                   Pr[VI (x) accepts |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩] ≥ c(n).

Soundness. If x ∈ Ino , then for every collection of unentangled states |ψ1 ⟩, · · · , |ψk ⟩, each on
    at most ℓ(n) qubits and with non-negative amplitudes, we have
                                   Pr[VI (x) accepts |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩] ≤ s(n).

Furthermore, c(n) and s(n) satisfy 1/2 ≤ s(n) < c(n) ≤ 1 and c(n) − s(n) ≥ 1/ poly(n). For
convenience, we write StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K to emphasize the promise gap ∆(n) := c(n)−s(n), abbre-
viate the stoquastic verification circuit VI (x) as Vx , and use the subscript 1 − ε in StoqMA(k )1−ε
to denote the case of completeness error at most ε.

Remark 3.2 (Optimal witness of any StoqMA(k ) verifier is non-negative). It is worth noting that
the restriction in Definition 3.1 to non-negative witness states does not change the computational
power of the class, because optimal stoquastic separable values can be attained by non-negative
states (Proposition 3.7). Although this phenomenon appears similar to that in the StoqMA
case, the underlying reason is different: in the StoqMA case, the conclusion follows from the
Perron-Frobenius theorem (e.g., [Liu21, Remark 2.2]).

Parallel repetition for StoqMA(k ). The main result of this section is that StoqMA(k ) admits
parallel-repetition-type phenomenon:

Theorem 3.3 (Parallel repetition for StoqMA(k )). For any efficiently computable functions
a(n) and b(n) with 0 ≤ b(n) < a(n) ≤ 1, and any efficiently computable positive integer-valued
functions k(n) and r(n) bounded by poly(n), the following inclusion holds:
                                                       1 (1 + a)r 1 (1 + b)r
                                                                              
                       1 a 1 b
           StoqMAℓ k, + , +           ⊆ StoqMArℓ k, +               , +           .
                       2 2 2 2                         2     2r+1 2        2r+1
    It is noteworthy that the proof technique underlying Theorem 3.3 extends to the more general
setting in which the parallel repetitions are associated with distinct StoqMA(k ) verifiers, and
hence with different promise problems. More specifically, given two promise problems I (1) :=
   (1)   (1)              (2)   (2) 
 Iyes , Ino and I (2) := Iyes , Ino , their direct product is defined by:
           (Iyes , Ino ) := I (1) × I (2) ,   where Iyes := Iyes
                                                             (1)    (2)
                                                                 × Iyes and Ino := Ino
                                                                                    (1)    (2)
                                                                                        × Ino  .
    Furthermore, we refer to the corresponding closure property of StoqMA(k ) in the parameter
regime of Theorem 3.3 as direct product with parameter loss, as stated in Theorem 3.14. Despite
the parameter loss, a direct calculation yields the following error-reduction consequence for
StoqMA(k ) when the completeness error is negligible:

Corollary 3.4 (Error reduction for StoqMA(k )1−negl ). Let δ(n) and b(n) be efficiently com-
putable non-negative functions with 0 ≤ b(n) < 1 − 1/poly(n) and b(n) < 1 − δ(n), and suppose
that δ(n) is negligible, and let k(n) and r(n) be efficiently computable positive integer-valued



                                                       16
functions that are polynomially bounded, where r(n) is chosen as the number of repetitions.
Then parallel repetition gives the following inclusion:
                                                            1 1 − δ ′ 1 δ ′′
                                                                          
                           1 1−δ 1 b
             StoqMAℓ k, +            , +       ⊆ StoqMArℓ k, +       , +      .

Here, the resulting threshold parameters are
                                   δ(n) r(n)

Furthermore, δ ′ (n) is negligible for every polynomially bounded r(n), while δ ′′ (n) is negligible
whenever r(n) is chosen sufficiently large. In particular, to guarantee the soundness error bound
δ ′′ (n) ≤ 2−n , it suffices to take

Remark 3.5 (Parameter loss in parallel repetition for StoqMA(k )). There is parameter loss in the
regime underlying Theorem 3.3, whereas some subclasses of StoqMA(k ) admit direct product
without parameter loss, as is already implicit in [Liu21, Section 5]. This closure property holds
for the subclass of StoqMA(k ) that we call ProdStoqMA(k ). The definition of ProdStoqMA(k )
is identical to that of Definition 3.1, except that for a ProdStoqMA(k ) verifier Vx , themaximum
acceptance probability ω(Vx ) has the product form ω(Vx ) = 1 + hSep+ (2ℓ ,··· ,2ℓ ) (Mx ) /2, where
Mx = ⟨0̄|⟨+̄|Vx† XO Vx |0̄⟩|+̄⟩ and Mx =
                                                     (j)                       (j)
                                              j∈[k] Mx . Here, each factor Mx is Hermitian and
                                           N
entrywise non-negative and corresponds to the j-th component of the witness state for j ∈ [k].
Consequently, ProdStoqMA(k ) admits parallel repetition without parameter loss. The detailed
statement and proof can be found in an earlier version of our work [LW26, Section 3.4].

3.2     Two StoqMA(k )-complete problems from the class definition
We introduce two StoqMA(k )-complete problems that arise directly from the class definition,
and each admits an interpretation in terms of property testing over different objects. Our
second complete problem (SepRCDk ) is directly analogous to the StoqMA-complete problem
Reversible Circuit Distinguishability (RCD) introduced in [Liu21, Section 4].

3.2.1     Separable Stoquastic Close Image to Uniform
We start with an observation due to [Kob03, Theorem 9]: deciding whether Tr(ρ0 ρ1 ) is at least
2/3 or at most 1/3 for single-qubit states ρ0 and ρ1 , whose purifications can be prepared effi-
ciently, is BQP-complete. This observation suggests that the acceptance probability of quantum
computation can be interpreted as a closeness testing problem. Moreover, when neither ρ0 nor ρ1
admits such a measurement, we can instead implement the measurement induced by the SWAP
test for mixed states [BCWdW01, KMY09], which accepts with probability (1 + Tr(ρ0 ρ1 ))/2.
    Since the SWAP test can be viewed as a stoquastic verifier, as implicitly observed in [Liu21,
Section 4], we introduce our first StoqMA(k )-complete problem SepStoqCIUk : 19
Definition 3.10 (k-Separable Stoquastic Close Image to Uniform, SepStoqCIUk ).
Let R be a polynomial-size classical reversible circuit consisting only of Toffoli, CNOT, and X
gates. Suppose that R acts on an m(n)-qubit input state of the form
                                          |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩ ⊗ |0⟩⊗m0 ⊗ |+⟩⊗m+ ,
where each |ψj ⟩ is an ℓ(n)-qubit non-negative state for every j ∈ [k], and kℓ + m0 + m+ = m.
Assume that R has r(n) designated output qubits, where 1 ≤ r(n) ≤ m(n) and r = r0 + r+ .
Let ΦR (·) denote the quantum channel obtained by applying R and then tracing out all non-
output qubits.20 The promise problem SepStoqCIUk [α(n), β(n)] is to decide whether one of the
following holds:
                                N                                              
                                                                   ⊗r0        ⊗r+
    • Yes:        max       F2 ΦR      j∈[k] |ψj ⟩⟨ψ j |   , |0⟩⟨0|    ⊗|+⟩⟨+|      ≥ α(n).
              |ψ1 ⟩,··· ,|ψk ⟩∈S+ (2ℓ )
                                            N                                       
                                                                        ⊗r0        ⊗r+
    • No:             max                F2 ΦR      |ψ
                                               j∈[k] j ⟩⟨ψ j |  , |0⟩⟨0|    ⊗|+⟩⟨+|      ≤ β(n).
             |ψ1 ⟩,··· ,|ψk ⟩∈S+ (2ℓ )

When k = 2, we simply write this problem as SepStoqCIU[α(n), β(n)].
    We then establish our first StoqMA(k )-complete problem:
Lemma 3.11 (SepStoqCIUk is StoqMA(k )-complete). For all efficiently computable functions
α(n) and β(n), and every efficiently computable positive integer-valued function k(n) ≤ poly(n),
such that 0 ≤ β(n) < α(n) ≤ 1 and α(n) − β(n) ≥ 1/poly(n), the following holds:
  19

with the maximization over |ψ⟩ ∈ S+ 22ℓ .

     Notably, the underlying quantum channel belongs to a subclass of completely positive trace-preserving maps

Proof. We prove only the case where k = 2 for simplicity, and the same argument directly
extends to general k. To establish Item (1), we simply consider the SWAP test [BCWdW01]
with two quantum states
                ρ0 := ΦR (|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 | ⊗ |ψ2 ⟩⟨ψ2 |) and ρ1 := |0⟩⟨0|⊗r0 ⊗ |+⟩⟨+|⊗r+ ,
which directly constitutes a StoqMA(2) verifier given the witness state |ψ1 ⟩⊗|ψ2 ⟩. Noting that ρ1
is a pure state, it follows that F2 (ρ0 , ρ1 ) = Tr(ρ0 ρ1 ). Consequently, the acceptance probability
of the SWAP test for mixed states [KMY09, Proposition 9] implies the desired threshold bound.
    To prove Item (2), we begin with a stoquastic verifier VI corresponding to the promise
problem I ∈ StoqMAℓ (2). For any x ∈ I, we fix the stoquastic verifier to be Vx := VI (x). For
any non-negative product state witness |ψ1 ⟩⊗|ψ2 ⟩, the acceptance probability of Vx is
                  Pr[Vx accepts|ψ1 ⟩⊗|ψ2 ⟩] = ∥|+⟩⟨+|O Vx (|ψ1 ⟩|ψ2 ⟩|+̄⟩|0̄⟩)∥22
                                             = Tr(|+⟩⟨+|O ΦR (|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |⊗|ψ2 ⟩⟨ψ2 |))
                                             = F2 (|+⟩⟨+|, ΦR (|ψ1 ⟩⟨ψ1 |⊗|ψ2 ⟩⟨ψ2 |)).
Here, the third line follows from the identity in Equation (2.1). Therefore, we complete the
proof by taking ΦR to be the channel defined by
                   ∀ρ ∈ D+ 22ℓ , ΦR (ρ) := TrO R(ρ ⊗ |0̄⟩⟨0̄| ⊗ |+̄⟩⟨+̄|)R† ,

where R = Vx is the required classical reversible circuit.

3.2.2   Separable Reversible Circuit Distinguishability
Next, we present our second StoqMA(k )-complete problem SepRCDk , which is a direct gener-
alization of [Liu21, Section 4]:
Definition 3.12 (k-Separable Reversible Circuit Distinguishability, SepRCDk ). Let
R0 and R1 be polynomial-size classical reversible circuits consisting only of Toffoli, CNOT, and
X gates. Suppose that both R0 and R1 act on an m(n)-qubit input state of the form |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗
|ψk ⟩ ⊗ |0⟩⊗m0 ⊗ |+⟩⊗r , where each |ψj ⟩ is an ℓ(n)-qubit non-negative state for every j ∈ [k]
and kℓ + m0 + r = m. With a slight abuse of notation, let |Rb ⟩ denote the resulting state,
defined by |Rb ⟩ := Rb (|ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩ ⊗ |0̄⟩ ⊗ |+̄⟩), for each b ∈ {0, 1}. The promise problem
SepRCDk [α(n), β(n)] is to decide whether one of the following holds:
   • Yes: There exists a non-negative state |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩ such that ⟨R0 |R1 ⟩ ≥ α(n).
   • No: For all non-negative states |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩, we have ⟨R0 |R1 ⟩ ≤ β(n).
When k = 2, we simply write this problem as SepRCD[α(n), β(n)], and we denote the version
with detailed input-size parameters by SepRCDα,β (ℓ, m0 , r).
   It is worth noting that, when k = 1, the promise problem SepRCD1 coincides with the
StoqMA-complete problem RCD introduced in [Liu21, Section 4.1].
Lemma 3.13 (SepRCDk is StoqMA(k )-complete). For all efficiently computable functions α(n)
and β(n), and every efficiently computable positive integer-valued function k(n) ≤ poly(n), such
that 0 ≤ β(n) < α(n) ≤ 1 and α(n) − β(n) ≥ 1/poly(n), we have
                                                                
                                                    1+α 1+β
                   SepRCDk [α, β] is StoqMA k,            ,        -complete.
                                                      2      2
In particular, for any stoquastic verification circuit Vx , the hard instance is given by the reversible
circuit pair R0 = Vx† XO Vx := Γx and R1 = I.

given in Appendix C.1.

4    Robustness of StoqMA(2)
In this section, we study the robustness of the definition of StoqMA(2) by establishing two
basic properties analogous to those of QMA(2), particularly prover compression [HM13] and
witness symmetrization [ABD+ 09]. Notably, while StoqMA(2) admits only parallel repetition
(Theorem 3.3), rather than full error reduction as in QMA(2), our results imply that StoqMA(2)
is well-defined with respect to these properties, up to a quadratic gap loss.

   When there are more than two provers, we establish prover compression for StoqMA(k )
by stoquastizing the well-known product test. This technique allows one to “compress” any
StoqMA(k ) protocol to a StoqMA(2) protocol while preserving the constant gap:

Theorem 4.1 (Prover compression for StoqMA(k ) up to a quadratic gap loss, informal version
of Theorem 4.7). Let k(n) be a polynomially bounded integer-valued function satisfying that
k(n) > 2. Let ε(n) and ∆(n) be functions such that 0 ≤ ε(n) < 1/2 and ∆(n) > 0. Then the
following inclusion holds:
                    StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAkℓ 2, 1 − O(ε∆), Ω(∆2 )
                                                    q                    y

    When the completeness error ε(n) is negligible, combining prover compression with parallel



                                                            23
repetition for StoqMA(2) (Theorem 3.3, more specifically Corollary 3.4) yields prover compres-
sion without any gap loss, and thus achieves a promise gap exponentially close to 1/2:21
Corollary 4.2 (Prover compression for StoqMA(k )1−negl ). Let k(n) be a polynomially bounded
integer-valued function satisfying k(n) > 2. Let ε(n) be a negligible non-negative function, and
let ∆(n) be a positive function that is at least 1/ poly(n). Then the following bounds hold:
 (1) There exist polynomially bounded integer-valued functions r(n) and q(n) such that
                                                        s                  {

                      StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMArkℓ 2, 1 − ε , − 2       .                           (4.1)

      letting ε⋆ ≤ ε/(2∆) and ℓ⋆ (n) = O(kℓ/∆), it holds that
                             StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAℓ⋆ J2, 1 − ε⋆ , Ω(∆)K.                        (4.2)

 (2) Without further parallel repetition, it holds that
                           StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAkℓ 2, 1 − ε/2, Ω(∆2 ) .                         (4.3)
