    In addition to prover compression, we also prove witness symmetrization for StoqMA(k ),
which can be viewed as a closure property under symmetrization of the product witness states.
More specifically, we define a symmetrized version of StoqMA(k ), denoted by SymStoqMA(k ),
in which all k factors (“proofs”) of the witness state are promised to be identical, as stated
in Definition 4.3. This symmetric variant is analogous to the class SymQMA(k) introduced
in [ABD+ 09, Section 4.4].
Definition 4.3 (SymStoqMA(k )). A promise problem I = (Iyes , Ino ) ∈ SymStoqMAℓ (k, c, s) if
there exists a stoquastic verifier VI such that for every input x ∈ I, the verifier is specified by
an n-qubit stoquastic verification circuit VI (x), as in Definition 3.1, such that the following hold
for efficiently computable functions c(n), s(n), and ℓ(n):
  21
     One subtlety behind Theorem 4.1 concerns the dyadic truncation error, as explained in Footnote 23, which
may prevent perfect completeness being preserved in the resulting StoqMA(2) verification circuit. Nevertheless,
together with parallel repetition for StoqMA(2) (Theorem 3.3), we can simply choose λ = 1/2, carry out the
remaining construction, and amplify the promise gap later.


                                                      24
Completeness.       If x ∈ Iyes , then there exists an ℓ(n)-qubit non-negative state |ψ⟩ such that
                                        h                     i
                                      Pr VI (x) accepts |ψ⟩⊗k ≥ c(n).

Soundness.      If x ∈ Ino , then for every ℓ(n)-qubit non-negative state |ψ⟩, we have
                                        h                   i
                                     Pr VI (x) accepts |ψ⟩⊗k ≤ s(n).

Furthermore, c(n) and s(n) satisfy 1/2 ≤ s(n) < c(n) ≤ 1 and c(n) − s(n) ≥ 1/ poly(n). For
convenience, we write the stoquastic verification circuit VI (x) as Vx .

    To establish the equivalence between StoqMA(k ) and SymStoqMA(k ) up to a quadratic gap
loss, we first show that SymStoqMA(k ) can simulate StoqMA(k ) using a new length-efficient
symmetrization, and then prove that StoqMA(k ) can simulate SymStoqMA(k ) by stoquastizing
the projector onto the symmetric subspace:

Theorem 4.4 (Witness symmetrization for StoqMA(k ) up to quadratic gap loss). Let k(n) be
a polynomially bounded integer-valued function satisfying that k(n) ≥ 2. Let ε(n) and ∆(n) be
functions such that 0 ≤ ε(n) < 1/2 and ∆(n) > 0. Then the following inclusions hold:

 (1) StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ SymStoqMAO(ℓ log k+log2 k) Jk, 1 − ε − ∆/2, ∆/2K.

 (2) SymStoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAℓ k, 1 − O(ε∆), Ω(∆2 ) .
                                       q                          y


    In the remainder of this section, we establish Theorem 4.1 in Section 4.1: we introduce
the stoquastic product test in Section 4.1.1, and then use it to prove prover compression for
StoqMA(k ) in Section 4.1.2. Next, we prove Theorem 4.4 in Section 4.2: we first present our
length-efficient symmetrization of StoqMA(k ) by SymStoqMA(k ) in Section 4.2.1, then introduce
the stoquastic equality test in Section 4.2.2, finally use it to show that StoqMA(k ) can simulate
SymStoqMA(k ) in Section 4.2.3.

4.1     StoqMA(k ) vs. StoqMA(2)
We begin by establishing a stoquastic version of the product test (Theorem 4.6), and then use
this procedure as a key ingredient in proving prover compression for StoqMA(k ).

4.1.1    The stoquastic product test
Lemma 4.5 (Stoquastic implementation of the product test). For every pair of pure states
              ℓ
|ρ⟩, |σ⟩ ∈ (C2 )⊗k , there is a stoquastic verification circuit that takes the state |ρ⟩ ⊗ |σ⟩ as input
and accepts with probability
                     1 1                                          1 X
                       + Pprod (ρ, σ), where Pprod (ρ, σ) := k           Tr(ρS σS ).               (4.4)
                     2 2                                         2
                                                                    S⊆[k]

In particular, if |ρ⟩ = |σ⟩ is a product state across the k tensor factors, then the circuit accepts
with probability 1.

Proof. Let ρ, σ be states over A = A1 · · · Ak and B = B1 · · · Bk , respectively, where each Ai and

Pprod (ρ, σ) is the probability that the standard product test accepts ρ and σ.

 Protocol 1: Stoquastic implementation of the product test.
   Registers      : J: the k-qubit branch-index register, initialized to |+⟩⊗k .

    the t-th qubit of Ai and the t-th qubit of Bi , controlled on the branch bit Ji .
    Equivalently, one can construct a reversible circuit Γ on (J, A, B, Z) satisfying22

   If |ρ⟩ = ki=1 |ρi ⟩ is product and |σ⟩ = |ρ⟩, then every reduced density matrix ρS is pure, so
Tr(ρ2S ) = 1 for all S. Hence Pprod (ρ, ρ) = 1, and the circuit accepts with probability 1.

Theorem 4.6 (Stoquastic product test). There exists an absolute constant cprod > 0 such that
for every pure k-partite state |ρ⟩, if

4.1.2   Prover compression for StoqMA(k )
An immediate consequence of the stoquastic product test (Theorem 4.6) is prover compression
for StoqMA(k ), which reduces the number of provers from k to two:

Theorem 4.7 (Prover compression for StoqMA(k ) up to a quadratic gap loss). Let k(n) be a
polynomially bounded integer-valued function satisfying k(n) > 2. Let the completeness error
ε(n) and the promise gap ∆(n) be functions such that 0 ≤ ε(n) < 1/2 and ∆(n) > 0. Then the
following inclusion holds:
                                                                  γ∆2
                                                       s              {
                      StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ StoqMAkℓ 2, 1 − λε,      .
                                                                   2
Here, γ := cprod /4, λ := γ∆, and cprod is the constant from Theorem 4.6, which may be taken
as 1/3. In particular, the resulting gap shows that our construction preserves a constant gap ∆.

   To establish the soundness, we also need the following simple proposition:

Proposition 4.8 (Product-state approximation preserves non-negativity). Let |ξ⟩ ∈ S+ (d1 · · · dk )
be a non-negative state, and let |Φ⟩ = |ϕ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ϕk ⟩ be a product state. For each i ∈ [k],
let ϕ+i  be obtained from |ϕi ⟩ by replacing every computational-basis amplitude by its absolute
value, and set |Φ+ ⟩ := ϕ+             +
                         1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ ϕk . Then

                                     T(|ξ⟩, Φ+ ) ≤ T(|ξ⟩, |Φ⟩).
In particular, if |Φ⟩ = |ψ⟩⊗k , then |Φ+ ⟩ = (|ψ + ⟩)⊗k .

Proof. Write |ξ⟩ = z ξz |z⟩ with ξz ≥ 0, and write |Φ⟩ = z Φz |z⟩. Then
                     P                                    P

                                        X               X
                            |⟨ξ|Φ⟩| =        ξz Φ z ≤       ξz |Φz | = ⟨ξ|Φ+ ⟩,
                                         z              z

which implies the desired bound by Equation (2.2).

   We then continue to prove Theorem 4.7:

Proof of Theorem 4.7. Let V be the given StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K verifier acting on witness regis-
ters A1 , . . . , Ak . Let c(n) := 1 − ε(n) and s(n) := c(n) − ∆(n) be the completeness and soundness
parameters of V , respectively. We now construct a new stoquastic verification circuit W with
witness registers A = A1 · · · Ak and B = B1 · · · Bk . The witness state has the form |Υ1 ⟩A ⊗ |Υ2 ⟩B ,
where |Υ1 ⟩ and |Υ2 ⟩ are called the first and second proof states, respectively, and each consists
of kℓ qubits. The verification circuit W proceeds as follows:

   • With probability 1 − λ, run the product-test branch from Protocol 1 on the two registers
     A and B.



                                                   27
   • With probability λ, ignore B and run the original verification circuit V on the k blocks
     A1 , . . . , Ak of the first proof state.
Since our protocol is a convex combination (1 − λ, λ) of two stoquastic verification circuits, the
resulting verification circuit remains stoquastic.23

Completeness. For yes instances, there exist pure states |ψ1 ⟩, . . . , |ψk ⟩ such that V accepts
|ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩ with probability at least c. Let
                                        |Ψ⟩ := |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩.
   The honest two-proof witness for W is |Ψ⟩A ⊗ |Ψ⟩B . The product-test branch accepts it
with probability 1, and the branch running V accepts with probability at least c. Hence the
completeness of W is c′ := 1 − λ(1 − c).

Soundness. Fix
            p an arbitrary product witness |ρ⟩A ⊗ |σ⟩B . Let η(ρ) be defined as in Theorem 4.6,
and set x := η(ρ). Then there exists a product state
                                         |Φ⟩ = |ϕ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ϕk ⟩
such that T(|ρ⟩, |Φ⟩) ≤ x. By Proposition 3.7 and Proposition 4.8, it suffices to consider a non-
negative product witness |ρ⟩A ⊗ |σ⟩B , and the product state |Φ⟩ satisfying T(|ρ⟩, |Φ⟩) ≤ x may
also be chosen non-negative. By the soundness s of V , the branch running V accepts |Φ⟩ with
probability at most s. By the measurement bound for trace distance (Lemma 2.3), together with
T(|ρ⟩, |Φ⟩) ≤ x, the same branch accepts |ρ⟩ with probability at most s + x. Combining this
reasoning with the trivial upper bound 1, its acceptance probability is at most min{1, s + x}.
By Theorem 4.6, the product-test branch accepts |ρ⟩A ⊗ |σ⟩B with probability at most 1 − γx2 .
Therefore,
                   Pr[W accepts |ρ⟩|σ⟩] ≤ (1 − λ)(1 − γx2 ) + λ min{1, s + x}.
Subtracting this from c′ yields
                  c′ − Pr[W accepts |ρ⟩|σ⟩] ≥ λ c − min{1, s + x} + (1 − λ)γx2 .                       (4.6)
                                                                 

It remains to simplify the lower bound in Equation (4.6):
   • If s + x ≤ 1, then Equation (4.6) gives
                       c′ − Pr[W accepts |ρ⟩|σ⟩] ≥ λ∆ − λx + (1 − λ)γx2 =: f (x).
      This is a convex quadratic whose unique minimizer is
                                                     λ          ∆
                                         x∗ :=             =          .
                                                 2(1 − λ)γ   2(1 − λ)
      Because λ = γ∆ ≤ γ < 1/2, we have x∗ ≤ ∆ ≤ 1 − s, so the minimizer lies within the
      regime s + x ≤ 1. Therefore, it follows that
                                              λ2                          γ∆2
                                                                     

                  f (x) ≥ f (x∗ ) = λ∆ −            = γ∆ 1 −            ≥     .
                                          4(1 − λ)γ          4(1 − λ)      2
   • If s + x ≥ 1, then Equation (4.6) yields
                       c′ − Pr[W accepts |ρ⟩|σ⟩] ≥ −λ(1 − c) + (1 − λ)γx2 =: g(x).
      The function g is increasing for x ≥ 0, and the boundary between the two regimes is
      x0 = 1 − s ≥ ∆ ≥ x∗ . Since g(x0 ) = f (x0 ), we obtain
                                                                         γ∆2
                                   g(x) ≥ g(x0 ) = f (x0 ) ≥ f (x∗ ) ≥       .
                                                                          2
  23
     The only subtlety is that the parameter λ ∈ (0, 1) should be a dyadic number representable with poly(n)
bits. We ignore the truncation error incurred by making λ dyadic, since this error is exponentially small and
does not significantly affect the completeness and soundness parameters.

   Therefore, in all cases the acceptance probability is at most c′ − γ∆

4.2     SymStoqMA(k ) vs. StoqMA(k )
We now establish the equivalence between StoqMA(k ) and SymStoqMA(k ) up to a quadratic
gap loss. We first show that SymStoqMA(k ) can simulate StoqMA(k ) in Section 4.2.1, and then,
after introducing the stoquastic equality test in Section 4.2.2, prove that StoqMA(k ) can simulate
SymStoqMA(k ) in Section 4.2.3.

4.2.1    StoqMA(k ) ⊆ SymStoqMA(k )
To prove that StoqMA(k ) ⊆ SymStoqMA(k ), as stated in Theorem 4.4(1), the simple diagonal
selection trick in [ABD+ 09, Lemma 4.8] would suffice: each prover in SymStoqMA(k ) sends the
concatenation of the k witness states |ψ1 ⟩|ψ2 ⟩ · · · |ψk ⟩ in the StoqMA(k ) protocol. Then the
SymStoqMA(k ) verifier will simulate the StoqMA(k ) verifier by taking only the i-th witness from
the i-th prover of SymStoqMA(k ). It follows that
                             StoqMAℓ Jk, c, sK ⊆ SymStoqMAℓk Jk, c, sK.                          (4.7)
The downside of the above trick is that the proof length blows up by a factor of k.

A length-efficient symmetrization. We provide a new simulation that only blows up the
proof length by a multiplicative factor O(log k) and a new additive O(log2 k) factor. The new
idea is that each symmetric prover can send a bundle of r = O(log  P k) copies of the coherent
superposition of the k proofs from the StoqMA(k ) provers, √1k j∈[k] |j⟩|ψj ⟩, instead of the
wasteful concatenation of the k proofs. Then by a coupon collector principle, a typical branch of
the k symmetric proofs, now consisting of O(k log k) random proofs from the set of k proofs of
StoqMA(k ), contains all k different proofs with high probability. Therefore the SymStoqMA(k )
verifier will be able to simulate the old StoqMA(k ) verifier in a typical branch. We start by
formalizing our specific coupon collector lemma in the language of perfect matching.

Theorem 4.9 (Hall’s theorem [Hal35]). A bipartite graph with left side L and right side R has
a matching of size |L| if and only if
                                 |N (S)| ≥ |S|     for every S ⊆ L,
where N (S) ⊆ R denotes the neighborhood of S in R.

Lemma 4.10 (Random matching bound). Let BN,d be the random bipartite multi-graph with left
side L and right side R, where L = R = [N ] and each left vertex independently chooses d right
vertices uniformly at random, with replacement. If d = ⌈12 ln N ⌉, then BN,d has a matching
covering all left vertices with probability at least 3/4.

Proof. By Hall’s theorem (Theorem 4.9), it suffices to rule out a set S ⊆ L with |N (S)| < |S|.
Fix s = |S|. If Hall fails for this S, then there is a set T ⊆ R of size s − 1 such that all ds choices
made by vertices in S land inside T . Therefore
                                                                        s − 1 ds
                                                                         
                                                          N     N
                     Pr[∃S : |S| = s, |N (S)| < s] ≤                              .
                                                          s    s−1        N
Summing over s = 1, . . . , N and using d = ⌈12 ln N ⌉, this total is less than 1/4. Hence Hall’s
condition holds with probability at least 3/4.

   With the above matching bound at our disposal, we prove our promised length-efficient
upgrade to Equation (4.7):



                                                  29
Theorem 4.11 (Length-efficient symmetrization). Let k(n) be a polynomially bounded integer-
valued function satisfying that k(n) ≥ 2. Let ε(n) and ∆(n) be functions such that 0 ≤ ε(n) < 1/2
and ∆(n) > 0, and set c(n) := 1 − ε(n). Let r := ⌈12 ln k⌉ and m := r(ℓ + ⌈log k⌉), which are
polynomially bounded integer-valued functions. Then the following inclusion holds:
                       StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K ⊆ SymStoqMAm Jk, c − ∆/2, ∆/2K.
Equivalently, the new verifier has completeness at least c − ∆/2 and soundness at most c − ∆.
Moreover, the new witness length is
                         m(n) = O((ℓ(n) + log k(n)) log k(n)) ≤ poly(n).

Proof. Fix an input x, and let V be the original k-witness verifier. For yes instances, there are

                                 Pr[V accepts |ψ1 ⟩⊗· · ·⊗|ψk ⟩] ≥ c.                            (4.8)
For no instances, every product of k non-negative ℓ-qubit states is accepted with probability at
most c − ∆.

Honest symmetric proof. One slot consists of a label register and an ℓ-qubit data register. The

Since different index-label branches J = (ji,h )i∈[k],h∈[r] ∈ [k]k×r do not interfere before the
verifier’s final measurement, it suffices to analyze each index-label branch J separately. For a
fixed index-label branch, the verifier deterministically constructs, whenever possible, a mapping
scheme from the available labeled data registers to the roles of the original verifier.
    We now describe the construction for a fixed index-label J. Define a bipartite graph GJ =
(L ⊔ R, EJ ), where the left side L = [k] corresponds to the witness copies, and the right side
R = [k] corresponds to the roles of the original verifier. Place an edge between i ∈ L and a ∈ R
if and only if the i-th witness copy, namely (ji,h )h∈[r] , contains at least one slot labeled by a,
that is, ji,h⋆ = a for some h⋆ ∈ [r]. If GJ has a perfect matching, then the verifier chooses the
lexicographically first perfect matching as the mapping scheme and routes the corresponding
data registers to V . Otherwise, it runs a fixed dummy verifier that accepts with probability
c − ∆. The resulting stoquastic verification circuit for the length-efficient symmetrization is
given in Protocol 2.

random index-label branch J,

Soundness. Assume x is a no instance, and let |Φ⟩ be any non-negative one-copy state used

has no perfect matching, the verifier accepts with probability c − ∆ in Protocol 2. If it has a
perfect matching, the verifier selects one data block from each of k distinct proof copies. After
discarding unused registers, the old verifier receives a product state
                                              ρ1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ ρk .
Here, each ρi is obtained by slicing a non-negative pure state h∈[r] ψji,h D over the discarded
                                                              N
                                                                            i,h
registers. Thus ρ1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ ρk is a convex combination of non-negative product pure states.
Consequently, by convexity and Proposition 3.7, the original soundness bound also holds for
arbitrary non-negative product mixed states. Thus the matching branch accepts with probability
at most c − ∆. Every fixed index-label branch therefore contributes acceptance probability at
most c − ∆. Averaging over label tables preserves the same bound. The new soundness is at


                                                     31
most c − ∆.
   Combining completeness and soundness, the new promise gap is
                                  (c − ∆/2) − (c − ∆) = ∆/2,
as claimed.

4.2.2   The stoquastic equality test
Let H be the single-proof Hilbert space and let Uπ denote the unitary that permutes the k
witness registers according to π ∈ Sk . The projector onto the fully symmetric subspace satisfies
                                                1 X
                                        Πsym =         Uπ .
                                               k!
                                                      π∈Sk

    The only minor subtlety is that the ideal construction would use a uniform superposition
branch over the k! permutations, but such a state cannot generally be prepared exactly from
the allowed |0⟩ and |+⟩ ancillary qubits, since k! need not be dyadic. We avoid this issue by
introducing a q-qubit dyadic branch register, initialized to |+⟩⊗q , and mapping its 2q basis
branches to permutations as evenly as possible.

Lemma 4.12 (Stoquastic projector onto the symmetric subspace). Let b ≥ 0 be an integer, let
K := k!, set q := ⌈log K⌉ + b and N := 2q , and write r := N mod K. For every pure state

                                            r(K − r)   K

Here, the second line uses the triangle inequality and the bound ⟨Φ|Uπt |Φ⟩ ≤ 1, and the third

                                   ζdyad (q) ≤ K/(4N ) ≤ 2−b−2 .
    Finally, if |Φ⟩ = |ψ⟩⊗k , then Uπt |Φ⟩ = |Φ⟩ for every t, so the acceptance probability is exactly
1, as desired.

A direct consequence of the stoquastic equality test (Lemma 4.12) is the other direction of
witness symmetrization, which establishes Theorem 4.4(2):

Theorem 4.14. Let k(n) be a polynomially bounded integer-valued function satisfying k(n) ≥ 2.

                                                                    ∆2

constant and inverse-polynomial gaps.

Proof. Let V be the given SymStoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K verifier acting on witness registers
A1 , · · · , Ak . Let c(n) := 1 − ε(n) and s(n) := c(n) − ∆(n) be the completeness and soundness
parameters of V , respectively. We now construct a new stoquastic verification circuit W , using
the same witness registers A1 , · · · , Ak as follows:

      • With probability 1 − λ, run the symmetry branch from Protocol 3 with parameters b :=
         log 16/∆2 , q := ⌈log(k!) + b⌉, and ξ := ξdyad (q).
                   

      • With probability λ, run the original verification circuit V .

Since our protocol is a convex combination (1 − λ, λ) of two stoquastic verification circuits, the
resulting verification circuit remains stoquastic.24

Completeness. For yes instances, the honest witness is of the form |ψ⟩⊗k . The symmetry branch
accepts with probability 1, and the original branch accepts with probability at least c = 1 − ε.
Hence the completeness is c′ = 1 − λ(1 − c).

5    The power of StoqMA(2) via distribution testing
In this section, we study the power of StoqMA(2) with short proofs. We first establish that,
        √
with O( n) unentangled proofs of length O(log n), a stoquastic verifier can certify NP languages,
with a constant promise gap:

Theorem 5.1. For any constant ε > 0, the following inclusion holds:
                                               √
                                          s                      {
                                                           1
                     NP ⊆ StoqMAO(log n) O( n), 1 − ε, − 2ε .
                                                           2
    In view of Theorems 3.3 and 4.14, it suffices to prove the following symmetric version:

Theorem 5.2. For any constant ε > 0, there exists a constant ∆ = Ω(1) for which the following
inclusion holds:                                q √              y
                      NP ⊆ SymStoqMAO(log n) O( n), 1 − ε, ∆ .



                                                35
                                                                                             √
     The symmetric protocol in Theorem 5.2 is, in spirit, analogous to the famous QMAlog ( n)
protocol of Aaronson, Beigi, Drucker, Fefferman, and Shor [ABD+ 09], as well as Chen–Drucker’s
improved protocol [CD10]. Those protocols, however, use non-stoquastic operations and there-
fore are not directly applicable here. We design a stoquastic protocol for Dinur’s PCP [Din07]
by leveraging Paninski’s uniformity test for probability distributions. One extra technical ben-
efit is that Paninski’s uniformity test is not intertwined with other tests, making our soundness
analysis more modular.
Remark 5.3 (Stoquastizing the uniformity test loses perfect completeness). Notably, there is
a tradeoff between the number of provers K and the completeness 1 − ε in Theorem 5.2: the
required value of K contains a multiplicative factor of log 1ε , which comes from the completeness
loss of the distributional uniformity test and is known to be necessary [DGPP18]. Consequently,
our stoquastic version of the uniformity test cannot achieve perfect completeness, but it can
achieve completeness as high as 1 − 2− polylog(n) , at the cost of slightly increasing the number of
                  √        e √n).
provers from O( n) to O(
   Following Theorem 4.7 and Remark 5.3, we obtain another corollary: for a stoquastic verifier,
                     e √n) suffice to certify NP languages. Applying parallel repetition for Sto-
two proofs of length O(
qMA(2) from Theorem 3.3, which gives error reduction when completeness error ε is negligible,
then yields

Theorem 5.4. For every ε(n) = Ω 2− polylog(n) , the following inclusion holds:
                                                
                                                 s                  {
                                                             1
                            NP ⊆ StoqMAO(     √
                                            e n)   2, 1 − ε,   − 2ε  .
                                                             2

    The same intuition from distribution testing also suggests a two-prover StoqMA(2) protocol
for NP languages with logarithmic-size witnesses and an inverse-polynomial gap. This result can
be viewed as a StoqMA(2) counterpart of the Blier–Tapp protocol [BT12]:
                                     s            {
                                                 1         1
Theorem 5.5. NP ⊆ StoqMAO(log n) 2, 1 − Θ 2 , Θ 2               .
                                                 n        n
    Scaling this up to polynomial-size proofs using a sufficiently structured PCP for NEXP, say
the one from [JW23, Theorem 7.4], one obtains that
                                          r                            z
                          NEXP ⊆ StoqMA 2, 1 − 2− poly(n) , 2− poly(n) .
   The reverse inclusion is straightforward. Following the standard convention, we call
StoqMA(2) with an exponentially small gap PreciseStoqMA(2). Therefore, together with the
easy inclusion PreciseQMA(2) ⊆ NEXP (see, e.g., [Per12]), we obtain:

Corollary 5.6. PreciseStoqMA(2) = NEXP.


   To establish the above theorems, we record Dinur’s PCP as our starting point.

Definition 5.7 (Dinur’s Gap Constraint Graph Problem, GapCG, adapted from [Din07, Defi-
nition 1.5]). Fix constants d ∈ N, Q ∈ N, and η > 0. An instance of (1, η)-GapCGd,Q consists
of a d-regular graph G = (V, E), an alphabet Σ of size Q, and for every undirected edge (u, v) ∈ E
a binary relation Ruv ⊆ Σ × Σ. The promise is that exactly one of the following holds:

   • Yes: There exists a labeling ι : V → Σ satisfying every edge constraint, i.e.
                                  (ι(u), ι(v)) ∈ Ruv ,    ∀(u, v) ∈ E;

   • No: Every labeling ι : V → Σ violates at least an η-fraction of the undirected edges.


                                                36
Theorem 5.8 (Dinur’s PCP Theorem, adapted from [Din07, Theorem 1.7]). There exist absolute
constants d, Q ∈ N and η > 0, together with a O(m)-time
                                              e         reduction from SAT instances of size
m to (1, η)-GapCGd,Q instances whose underlying constraint graph G = (V, E) has size O(m),
                                                                                     e
meaning that |V | = O(m)
                    e    and |E| = O(m).
                                     e

                                                                        √
   In the remainder of this section, we first give a SymStoqMAO(log n) ( n) protocol for (1, η)-
GapCG in Section 5.1, which proves Theorem 5.2. We then apply the same intuition to obtain
a more direct StoqMAO(log n) (2) protocol with an inverse-polynomial promise gap in Section 5.2,
which proves Theorem 5.5.
                  √
5.1   A SymStoqMA( n) protocol for (1, η)-GapCG
                                    √
We begin by designing a SymStoqMA(O( n)) protocol that certifies NP languages with a con-
stant promise gap:

Theorem 5.2 (restated). For any constant ε > 0, there exists a constant ∆ = Ω(1) for which
the following inclusion holds:
                                                q √              y
                          NP ⊆ SymStoqMAO(log n) O( n), 1 − ε, ∆ .

    We will need the following classical theorem of Paninski [Pan08], in the clean formulation
recorded in [Can20, Theorem 5.1], to analyze the product weights induced by the witnesses:

Theorem 5.9 (Uniformity test for distributions, adapted from [Can20, Theorem 5.1]). Given
sample access to an unknown distribution q over a finite set Ω of size n. For every fixed δ > 0
and ε > 0, there is an efficiently computable predicate T on
                                              √          
                                                  n     1
                                       K=O          log
                                                 δ2     ε
i.i.d. samples from q, such that

   • If q is the uniform distribution, T accepts w.p. at least 1 − ε;
   • If q is δ-far from uniform in total variation distance, T accepts w.p. at most ε.

Furthermore, T depends only on the collision pattern among the samples.

    Given a GapCG instance I, we identify V × Σ with a subset of the computational basis on
⌈log |V |⌉ + ⌈log |Σ|⌉ = O(log |V |) qubits. Suppose the input is a yes instance with a satisfying
labeling ι : V → Σ, and let |V | = n. The honest one-copy witness is the subset state
                                               1 X
                                      |ϕL ⟩ = p         |v, ι(v)⟩.
                                               |V | v∈V
                                                                   √
Fix a constant C > 0, to be specified later, and let K := ⌈C n⌉. We ask for K copies of this
state, namely |Φ⟩ := |ϕL ⟩⊗K , so that the witnesses encode the satisfying labeling.

Branch-local protocol for vertex-label branches. Let Wi be the register containing the
i-th witness factor, and let W := (W1 , · · · , WK ). An arbitrary non-negative one-copy state |ψ⟩

    We refer to (vi , ai ) as the vertex-label factor branch in the i-th tensor factor, or simply as the

never measures W1 , · · · , WK before the final Hadamard-basis measurement. The terminology
serves only to identify the computational-basis branches on which the classical reversible circuit
is coherently evaluated.

This product-weight identity is the only sense in which distribution testing enters the proof.

                                                      X

   We are now ready to state the branch-local protocol, presented in Protocol 4, which describes
the behavior of the SymStoqMA(K) verifier within a fixed K-vertex-label branch, including
branch-local versions of the uniformity and consistency tests.

 Protocol 4: Branch-local protocol for NP on a fixed K-vertex-label branch
   1. Consider a K-vertex-label branch of |Ψ⟩ = |ψ⟩⊗K , denoted by

                                        ((v1 , a1 ), . . . , (vK , aK )) ∈ (V × Σ)K .

   2. Apply both the uniformity and consistency test :
       2.1 Uniformity test: Let T be the predicate from Paninski’s uniformity tester
        in Theorem 5.9, distinguishing the uniform distribution µV over V from
        distributions q such that TV(q, µV ) ≥ δ, for some fixed distance parameter δ > 0
        with error parameter ε. Apply the deterministic collision-based predicate T to the
        vertex components v1 , . . . , vK .
       2.2 Consistency test: Accept if neither of the following occurs
         (i) Exist i ̸= j such that vi = vj but ai ̸= aj ;
        (ii) Exist i ̸= j such that (vi , vj ) ∈ E and (ai , aj ) ∈
                                                                  / Rvi vj .

   3. Accept if and only if both tests accept.



Implementing the branch-local protocol. To implement Protocol 4 as a stoquastic veri-
fication circuit, we consider the predicate Aunif underlying Paninski’s uniformity tester (Theo-
rem 5.9) and write the acceptance condition of the consistency test as the predicate Acons . Since
both predicates are deterministic, we obtain a deterministic predicate
                                                Abranch := Aunif ∧ Acons ,
which corresponds to the acceptance condition of Protocol 4.
    Let A0 and F denote the register containing |0⟩-ancillary qubits and a single-qubit register
initialized to |0⟩. Using the standard reversible-computation convention, we obtain a classical



                                                                 38
reversible circuit Γbranch satisfying that
                 ∀x ∈ (V × Σ)K ,    Γbranch |x⟩W |0̄⟩A0 |0⟩F = |x⟩W |0̄⟩A0 |Abranch (x)⟩F .
    Finally, we obtain the resulting stoquastic verification circuit by applying the branch-overlap
test (Lemma 2.13) to the circuit pair (Γbranch , I) on the input state |Ψ⟩W |0̄⟩A0 |0⟩F , where |Ψ⟩
equals |Φ⟩ for yes instances.

5.1.1     Analysis of Protocol 4
Now we analyze our branch-local verifier. Only the one-copy squared-amplitude distribution p
over V × Σ, as defined in Equation (5.1), matters, because the K-factor branch weights factor
as p⊗K , which corresponds, from the distribution testing perspective, to K i.i.d. samples drawn
from p. The vertex marginal q over V also has been defined in Equation (5.2).

Parameter choices. There are several running parameters in our analysis,
        ε, is the target error parameter in Protocol 4, which is positive and can be arbitrarily
        small;
        η, is the fixed constant from Dinur’s PCP Theorem 5.8, representing the minimum fraction
        of violating edges for any labeling in a no instance;
        δ, is the distance parameter used in Protocol 4, which we set to δ = η/48;
        κ, an auxiliary parameter, which we set to κ = η/64.

Completeness. For yes instances, the induced vertex distribution q is uniform, and the con-
sistency test is passed trivially with probability 1. Overall, the verifier accepts with probability
                                          √
at least 1 − ε for large enough K = O( n) by Theorem 5.9, where ε > 0 can be an arbitrarily
small constant, or even as small as 2− polylog(n) if we allow K = O(e √n). Hence,

Lemma 5.10 (Completeness of Protocol 4). The completeness of the classical branch predicate
is at least 1 − ε, and consequently, the completeness of the branch-local verifier V is at least
1 − ε/2.

Soundness. We now prove the soundness:
Lemma 5.11 (Soundness of Protocol 4). The soundness of the classical branch predicate is at
most ε, and consequently, the completeness of the branch-local verifier V is at most 1/2 + ε/2.
   If q is δ-far from the uniform distribution µV , the uniformity test rejects with probability
1 − ε. Hence, assume from now on that TV(q, µV ) < δ. Define the following sets
                               1
                                       H := v ∈ V q(v) > n2 ,
                                            
        L := v ∈ V q(v) < 2n     ,                                   G := V \ (L ∪ H).
Then because every v ∈ L contributes more than 2n      1
                                                          to the total deficit from µV , one has
|L| < 2δn; and analogously because every v ∈ H contributes more than n1 to the total excess
over µV , one has |H| < δn. Therefore, |V \ G| < 3δn.
    For each vertex v with q(v) > 0, define the conditional label distribution rv : Σ → R and the
plurality label ι∗ : V → Σ, that
                                        p(v, a)
                            rv (a) :=           ,     ι∗ (v) := arg max rv (a).
                                         q(v)                        a∈Σ

For vertices with q(v) = 0, choose ι∗ (v) arbitrarily. Define the local ambiguity
                                            bv := 1 − rv (ι∗ (v)).
Thus bv = 0 iff the label at v is deterministic. We split the soundness analysis into two cases.

                                                     39
 (a) There is substantial ambiguity on good vertices. Then the consistency test is likely to
     see a same vertex sampled twice with different labels by the birthday paradox, and thus
     rejects.

 (b) The label is almost deterministic on a good fraction of vertices. Then the consistency test
     is likely to see adjacent vertices with unsatisfying labels by the birthday paradox, and thus
     rejects.

    The following generalized birthday paradox, whose proof is deferred to Appendix C.2, cap-
tures the birthday-paradox estimate required by the two cases:

Lemma 5.12 (A generalized birthday paradox). Let Ω be a finite set of size n, µ be a probability
distribution on Ω, and B ⊆ Ω × Ω be a symmetric relation representing the “birthday collisions.”
For some subset Ω0 ⊆ Ω, if for some parameters α, β > 0 and some integer D > 0, the following
are true

 (1) µ(x) ≤ α/n for every x ∈ Ω0 ;
 (2) for every x ∈ Ω0 , the number of y ∈ Ω0 with (x, y) ∈ B is at most D;
 (3) the bad-pair weight on Ω0 satisfies
                                                X                          β
                                  λ :=                        µ(x)µ(y) ≥     .
                                                                           n
                                          (x,y)∈B∩(Ω0 ×Ω0 )

Then for any constant ε > 0, there is a large enough C such that the following holds for all
                                                                             √
sufficiently large n: Let X1 , . . . , XK be i.i.d. samples from µ with K = C n,
                        h                                              i
                      Pr ∃i < j such that (Xi , Xj ) ∈ B ∩ (Ω0 × Ω0 ) ≥ 1 − ε.

   With this generalized birthday paradox at our disposal, we prove the promised soundness of
our protocol by considering the aforementioned two cases:

                                      X

   Since we are in a no instance, the plurality labeling ι∗ violates at least an η-fraction of the
edges, i.e., at least η|E| = ηdn/2 edges. Delete all edges incident to V \ T . Since the graph has
degree d, the number of deleted edges is at most
                                          d|V \ T | < 3δdn + 4κdn.
   Now our choice of parameters δ = η/48 and κ = η/64 guarantees that there remain at least

 (i) both u and v lie in T ;
(ii) (ι∗ (u), ι∗ (v)) ∈
                      / Ruv .

Call these good violated edges. Now define the set

5.2   A StoqMAlog (2) protocol with inverse-polynomial gap
We now present the promised inverse-polynomial-gap version. In the constant-gap protocol,
                                                                               √
corresponding to its branch-local version presented in Protocol 4, the K = Θ( n) vertex-label
factor branches are used only to ensure that the relevant vertex- and edge-detection events have
constant total branch weight. With two vertex-label factor branches, the same events have
branch weight Θ(1/n), which is sufficient to obtain an inverse-polynomial promise gap.

The simple branch-local protocol and its stoquastic implementation. Similar to the
analysis in the previous subsection, it suffices to first consider the simple branch-local protocol
W , analogous to Protocol 4, as presented in Protocol 5.

 Protocol 5: Simple branch-local protocol for NP on a fixed 2-vertex-label branch.
  1. Consider a 2-vertex-label branch of |Ψ⟩ = |ψ⟩ ⊗ |ψ⟩, denoted by

                                     ((u, a), (v, b)) ∈ (V × Σ)2 .

   2. Apply either the uniformity or the consistency test, each with probability 1/2 :
       2.1 Uniformity test: Reject if u = v.
       2.2 Consistency test: Reject if either of the following occurs:
         (i) u = v and a ̸= b;
        (ii) (u, v) ∈ E and (a, b) ∈
                                   / Ruv .

   3. Accept if and only if the chosen test accepts.

    To implement Protocol 5 as a stoquastic verification circuit, we write the two acceptance
conditions of the uniformity and consistency tests, respectively, as deterministic predicates Aunif
and Acons , and implement them by classical reversible circuits Γunif and Γcons , respectively, using
the standard reversible-computation convention.

                                                 42
    Finally, we implement the convex combination (1/2, 1/2) of the two tests by combining
these circuits using an additional |+⟩ ancillary qubit, obtaining a reversible circuit Γbranch for
the uniform mixture of Γunif and Γcons . The resulting stoquastic verification circuit is then
obtained by applying the branch-overlap test (Lemma 2.13) to the circuit pair (Γbranch , I) with
the input state |+⟩|Ψ⟩|0̄⟩, where |Ψ⟩ coincides with |ϕL ⟩ ⊗ |ϕL ⟩ for yes instances.

Analysis of the branch-local verifier W . We analyze the branch-local protocol W on
two symmetric witnesses. From the distributional testing perspective, (u, a), (v, b) are drawn
from p⊗2 , where p is a distribution on V × Σ. We will use the same notation as in the proof
of Theorem 5.2.

6     Upper bounds for StoqMA(k ) with logarithmic-size proofs
In this section, we provide nearly tight upper bounds for StoqMA(k ) with logarithmic-size proofs.
We first prove that StoqMAlog collapses to BPP, which provides positive evidence for the long-
standing open problem of whether StoqMA = MA, since MAlog = BPP (e.g., [Reg06, Theorem
3]), and aligns with the equivalence QMAlog = BQP [MW05, Theorem 3.10]:
Theorem 6.1 (Randomized singly exponential-time upper bound for StoqMA). For all effi-
ciently computable functions c(n) and ∆(n) with c(n) ∈ (1/2, 1], ∆(n) ∈ (0, 1/2], and c(n) −
∆(n) ≥ 1/2, and for every efficiently computable positive integer-valued function ℓ(n) ≤ poly(n),
the following inclusion holds:
                                                     3ℓ                  
                      StoqMAℓ J1, c, ∆K ⊆ BPTIME O   e 2       · poly(|V |)   .
                                                         ∆2
Here, |V | denotes the description size of the underlying stoquastic verification circuit V .
Furthermore, up to lower-order factors, this upper bound is tight with respect to the proof length
ℓ under randomized ETH.
    By taking ℓ(n) = O(log n) in Theorem 6.1, we immediately obtain:
Corollary 6.2. StoqMAlog = BPP.
   For multiple-prover scenarios, although the best known upper bound for QMA(2)log is the
same as that for general QMA(2), namely NEXP, we establish an upper bound StoqMA(2)log ⊆
MA that is better than the bound due to StoqMA(2) ⊆ StoqMA [GJ26]:
Theorem 6.3 (StoqMA(k ) with logarithmic-size proofs is in MA). For all efficiently computable
functions c(n) and ∆(n) with 1/2 < c(n) ≤ 1, ∆(n) ≥ 1/ poly(n), and c(n) − ∆(n) ≥ 1/2,
and for every efficiently computable positive integer-valued function ℓ(n) and k(n) such that
ℓ(n) ≤ O(log n) and k(n) ≤ poly(n), the following inclusion holds:
                                    StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K ⊆ MA.
    In the remainder of this section, we first investigate the single-prover case in Section 6.1,
and then explore the multi-prover case in Section 6.2. Throughout this section, for convenience,
we slightly abuse notation and also use Γ to denote the exponential-size matrix induced by the
reversible circuit in an RCD or SepRCD instance.

6.1   StoqMAlog ⊆ BPP and general rETH-optimal BPTIME upper bound
The optimality, with respect to the proof length ℓ, of the BPTIME upper bound underlying
Theorem 6.1 follows from Lemma 6.4, and the proof is deferred to Appendix C.3.
Lemma 6.4 (Proof-length optimality under randomized ETH). Let ℓ(n) be an efficiently com-
putable positive integer-valued function satisfying ℓ(n) ≤ poly(n). Assume that
                                                                      
                        StoqMAℓ J1, 1, 1/2K ⊆ BPTIME 2o(ℓ) · poly(|V |) ,

                                               44
where |V | denotes the description size of the underlying stoquastic verification circuit V . Then
this randomized upper bound refutes randomized ETH.

6.1.1    Proof of Theorem 6.1
To establish the BPTIME upper bound for StoqMA, the key observation is that, once a
computational-basis witness state |a⟩ is fixed, with a ∈ {0, 1}ℓ , the only randomness remaining
in the compressed matrix MΓ comes from the |+⟩ ancillary qubits. Consequently, one random
simulation of Γ produces one sample from a column of MΓ . Estimating all 2ℓ columns takes
running time singly exponential in ℓ, which is polynomial in n when ℓ(n) = O(log n). We now
proceed with the detailed randomized algorithm and the corresponding analysis:

Proof of Theorem 6.1. Following Lemma 3.13, we consider a RCD instance (Γ, I) such that
                                 ⟨R0 (ψ)|R1 (ψ)⟩ = ⟨ψ|⟨0̄|⟨+̄|Γ|ψ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩
for every non-negative state |ψ⟩. Therefore, it suffices to distinguish
                 I + M                          I + M 
                         Γ                                 Γ
            λmax            ≥ c(n) from λmax                   ≤ s(n) := c(n) − ∆(n).
                     2                                  2
Here, MΓ := ⟨0|⊗m0 ⟨+|⊗r Γ|0⟩⊗m0 |+⟩⊗r is a matrix of size 2ℓ × 2ℓ , which is polynomial in n when
ℓ(n) ≤ O(log n). The rETH-based optimality statement follows from Lemma 6.4.

 Algorithm 1: Column-sampling matrix reconstruction of MΓ .
   Input          : A reversible circuit Γ and an entrywise accuracy parameter η > 0.
   Output         : An explicit matrix M fΓ approximating MΓ .
   Parameters: The witness length ℓ, the number of |0⟩ ancillary qubits m0 , and the
                    number of |+⟩ ancillary qubits r.
 1 Set L := {0, 1}ℓ and U := {0, 1}r .
 2 Set T := C log(20|L|2 )/η 2 for a sufficiently large absolute constant C.
                                

 3 Initialize an |L| × |L| zero matrix M  fΓ .
 4 For each column a ∈ L :
 5     Initialize counters Nb,a := 0 for all b ∈ L.
 6     For each sample j ∈ {1, . . . , T } :
 7         Sample u ∼ Unif(U ).
 8         Compute Γ(a, 0m0 , u) = (b, z, u′ ) ∈ L × {0, 1}m0 × U .
 9         If z = 0m0 :
10              Update Nb,a ← Nb,a + 1.
11     For each row b ∈ L :
12         Set (MfΓ )b,a := Nb,a /T .
13 Return M fΓ .


    To this end, we provide a column-sampling matrix reconstruction procedure for MΓ , as
presented in Algorithm 1. By invoking Algorithm 1 with the circuit Γ and the accuracy param-
eter η = ∆/(8|L|), we obtain M fΓ by estimating MΓ column by column. For every fixed pair
(a, b) ∈ L × L, define the Bernoulli random variable
                     Ya,b (u) := I[Γ(a, 0m0 , u) ∈ {b} × {0m0 } × U ],   where u ∈ U.
It is easy to verify that the expectation of Ya,b corresponds to the matrix entry ⟨b|MΓ |a⟩:
            E    [Ya,b (u)] = 2−r |{u ∈ U | Γ(a, 0m0 , u) ∈ {b} × {0m0 } × U }| = ⟨b|MΓ |a⟩.   (6.1)
        u∼Unif(U )

6.2     StoqMA(k )log ⊆ MA
We start with the two-prover case as a warm-up, and then move on to the multiple-prover case
with at most polynomially many provers.

6.2.1    A warm-up: StoqMA(2)log ⊆ MA

Since the full witness space has dimension 22ℓ = poly(n), after flattening the pair of witness
registers into one alphabet of size 22ℓ , Arthur can invoke Algorithm 1 exactly as in the one-
prover scenario. Nevertheless, the stoquastic separable value does not coincide with the largest
eigenvalue in general, the prover thus must provide a classical dyadic description of a rounded
separable witness, and Arthur evaluates that candidate product state against the reconstructed
matrix. We then continue with the actual proof:



                                               46
Theorem 6.5 (StoqMA(2) with logarithmic-size proofs is in MA). For all efficiently computable
functions c(n) and ∆(n) with 1/2 < c(n) ≤ 1, ∆(n) ≥ 1/ poly(n), and c(n) − ∆(n) ≥ 1/2, and
for every efficiently computable positive integer-valued function ℓ(n) such that ℓ(n) = O(log n),
the following inclusion holds:
                                     StoqMAℓ J2, c, ∆K ⊆ MA.

Proof. Using Lemma 3.13, we consider a SepRCD instance (Γ, I) such that
                                    ⟨R0 (Ψ)|R1 (Ψ)⟩ = ⟨Ψ|⟨0̄|⟨+̄|Γ|Ψ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩

            a′ , b′ MΓ |a, b⟩ = 2−r u ∈ U Γ(a, b, 0m0 , u) ∈ {a′ } × {b′ } × {0m0 } × U .  (6.5)

The general k-prover proof follows the same dyadic rounding procedure used in the two-prover
case (Theorem 6.5), but avoids reconstructing the full matrix MΓ , whose dimension may already
be exponential. Instead, Arthur directly estimates a single overlap using the dual-access viewpoint
from distribution testing [CR14, Theorem 6], following an approach similar to that used to
establish eStoqMA ⊆ MA [Liu21, Section 3].

Theorem 6.3 (StoqMA(k ) with logarithmic-size proofs is in MA, restated). For all efficiently
computable functions c(n) and ∆(n) with 1/2 < c(n) ≤ 1, ∆(n) ≥ 1/ poly(n), and c(n)−∆(n) ≥
1/2, and for every efficiently computable positive integer-valued function ℓ(n) and k(n) such that
ℓ(n) ≤ O(log n) and k(n) ≤ poly(n), the following inclusion holds:
                                         StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K ⊆ MA.

Proof. Following Lemma 3.13, we consider a SepRCDk instance (Γ, I) such that
                                ⟨R0 (Ψ)|R1 (Ψ)⟩ = ⟨Ψ|⟨0̄|⟨+̄|Γ|Ψ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩
for every non-negative state |Ψ⟩ := |ψ1 ⟩ ⊗ · · · ⊗ |ψk ⟩. Write L := {0, 1}ℓ and U := {0, 1}r . We
can define the matrix MΓ , indexed by (a1 , . . . , ak ), (b1 , . . . , bk ) ∈ Lk , which directly generalizes
Equation (6.5). Let QΓ := (I + MΓ )/2. It thus suffices to distinguish the stoquastic value
              hSep+ [k;2ℓ ] (QΓ ) ≥ c(n) from hSep+ [k;2ℓ ] (QΓ ) ≤ s(n) := c(n) − ∆(n).

Here, the entry-wise non-negative matrix QΓ is of size 2kℓ × 2kℓ , which is no longer polynomial
in n when ℓ(n) = O(log n), and satisfies 0 ⪯ QΓ ⪯ I.

Estimating the stoquastic separable value via dual access to the witness state. Not-
ing that ⟨Ψ′ |QΓ |Ψ′ ⟩ = (1 + ⟨Ψ′ |MΓ |Ψ′ ⟩)/2, it suffices to estimate the overlap ⟨Ψ′ |MΓ |Ψ′ ⟩. Our
approach is inspired by the framework underlying the proof of eStoqMA ⊆ MA [Liu21, Section 3],
which boils down to the dual-access viewpoint from distribution testing [CR14].
     The starting point is that both query and sample access to |Ω′ ⟩ can be implemented efficiently
from the classical witness (m1 , · · · , mk ), where the initialized rounded witness |Ω′ ⟩ := |Ψ′ ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩.
Implementing the query access to |Ω′ ⟩ follows immediately from the tensor-product structure of
|Ψ′ ⟩ and the fact that mi serves as a classical description of |ψi′ ⟩ whose amplitudes are dyadic


                                                       49
before normalization. To implement the sample access to |Ω′ ⟩, for each proof i ∈ [k], let Di be
the squared-amplitude distribution of |ψi′ ⟩, with the associated random variable Xi . Then,
                             mi (a)2                                X

                              Zi

Consequently, one can sample from the distribution Di exactly:

 (1) Draw an integer Wi ∼ Unif([Zi ]) using O(log Zi ) random bits;

 (2) Identify the unique ai ∈ L such that b<ai mi (b)2 < Wi ≤ b≤ai mi (b)2 .

   Since the lists mi are explicit and Zi has polynomial bit length, this sampling procedure
runs in probabilistic polynomial time. By sampling all k proofs independently in this way and
then sampling u ∼ Unif(U ), one can implement the sample access to |ψi′ ⟩.

7     Upper bounds for StoqMA(k ) with nearly perfect completeness
In this section, we establish upper bounds for StoqMA(k ) with nearly perfect completeness,
which are stronger than the general deterministic exponential-time upper bound for StoqMA(k )
stated in Theorem 8.2 and proved in Section 8 via the Sum-of-Squares algorithm of Barak–
Kelner–Steurer [BKS14]:

Theorem 7.1 (StoqMA(k ) with doubly exponentially small completeness error is in PSPACE).
Let ε(n) and s(n) be efficiently computable functions satisfying 0 ≤ ε(n) ≤ exp(− exp(poly(n)))
and 1/2 ≤ s(n) ≤ 1 − 1/ poly(n), and define ∆(n) := 1 − ε(n) − s(n). Then, for all efficiently
computable positive integer-valued functions k(n), ℓ(n), m0 (n), and m+ (n), it holds that:
                                        kℓ                   kℓ                      
   StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ DTISP exp O   e      max{kℓ, m+ } , O         max{kℓ, m0 , m+ } .
                                             ∆2                      ∆2
In particular, for every efficiently computable integer-valued function k that is polynomially
bounded in n, and for functions ε and s satisfying the above conditions, we have:
                                   StoqMA(k, 1 − ε, s) ⊆ PSPACE.

Theorem 7.2 (PreciseStoqMA(k ) with triply exponentially small completeness error is in EXP).
Let ε(n) and s(n) be efficiently computable functions such that
        0 ≤ ε(n) ≤ exp(− exp(exp(poly(n))))         and    1/2 ≤ s(n) ≤ 1 − 1/ exp(poly(n)),
and define ∆(n) := 1 − ε(n) − s(n). Then, for all efficiently computable positive integer-valued
functions k(n), ℓ(n), and m+ (n), the following inclusion holds:
                                               h                              i
               StoqMAℓ Jk, 1 − ε, ∆K ⊆ DTISP exp O(max{kℓ,       m+ }) , O 2kℓ .
                                                                      
                                                     e

In particular, for every efficiently computable integer-valued function k that is polynomially
bounded in n, and for functions ε and s satisfying the above conditions, it holds that:
                                  PreciseStoqMA(k, 1 − ε, s) ⊆ EXP.


   In the remainder of this section, we first introduce the rectangular structure of non-negative
product states in Section 7.1, via the StoqMA(2)-complete problem SepRCD, and then explain
how to test rectangular closure. Next, we provide two implementations of the formal algorithm
(Algorithm 2) together with their performance guarantees (Theorem 7.7) in Section 7.2, and
show how the complexity-theoretic consequences in Theorems 7.1 and 7.2 are derived. Lastly,
we prove the correctness of the rectangular closure testing algorithm in Section 7.3.

7.1    Rectangular structure of non-negative product states
We begin by describing the rectangular structure of non-negative witness states underlying yes
instances of StoqMA(2) with perfect completeness. In particular, we consider the StoqMA(2)-
complete problem SepRCD, introduced in Section 3.2.2.

Definition 7.3 (Perfectly agreeing pair). For any SepRCD instance (R0 , R1 ), we say that
(R0 , R1 ) is a perfectly agreeing pair if there exists a non-negative product state |ψ⟩ such that
      ⟨R0 (ψ)|R1 (ψ)⟩ = 1,     where |Rz (ψ)⟩ := Rz |ψ⟩|0⟩⊗m0 |+⟩⊗r for each z ∈ {0, 1}.            (7.1)
Furthermore, we say that (R0 , R1 ) is an ε-near perfectly agreeing pair if the condition in Equa-
tion (7.1) is relaxed to ⟨R0 (ψ)|R1 (ψ)⟩ ≥ 1 − ε.

Lemma 7.4 (Perfectly agreeing pairs admit product subset states). For every perfectly agreeing
pair (R0 , R1 ) corresponding to a yes instance of SepRCD1,β (ℓ, m0 , r), let |ψ ⋆ ⟩ := |ψ1⋆ ⟩ ⊗ |ψ2⋆ ⟩ be


                                                   52
a non-negative state that satisfies ⟨R0 (ψ ⋆ )|R1 (ψ ⋆ )⟩ = 1. Then there exist non-empty subsets
                             S1 ⊆ supp(|ψ1⋆ ⟩)       and     S2 ⊆ supp(|ψ2⋆ ⟩)
such that the product subset state |ϕ⟩ := |S1 ⟩ ⊗ |S2 ⟩ satisfies ⟨R0 (ϕ)|R1 (ϕ)⟩ = 1.

Proof. Following Lemma 3.13, it suffices to consider a SepRCD instance (Γ, I) such that
⟨R0 (ψ)|R1 (ψ)⟩ = ⟨ψ|⟨0̄|⟨+̄|Γ|ψ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩ for every non-negative state |ψ⟩. We now write
               X                          X
      |ψ1⋆ ⟩ =   αx(1)
                    1
                       |x1 ⟩ and |ψ2⋆ ⟩ =    αx(2)
                                                2
                                                   |x2 ⟩, where αx(1)
                                                                   1
                                                                      ≥ 0 ∀x1 and αx(2)
                                                                                     2
                                                                                        ≥ 0 ∀x2 .
               x1                        x2

   For the non-negative states |ψ1⋆ ⟩ and |ψ2⋆ ⟩, we define their supports by
                           n               o                 n              o
                     S1 := x1 αx(1)  1
                                       > 0       and  S 2 :=  x 2  α (2)
                                                                     x2  > 0 .

Thus, the support of the initialized state is the product set K := S1 × S2 × {0m0 } × U . Since
⟨Ω⋆ |Γ|Ω⋆ ⟩ = 1, where |Ω⋆ ⟩ := |ψ ⋆ ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩, a direct calculation shows that
         ∥|Ω⋆ ⟩ − Γ|Ω⋆ ⟩∥22 = ⟨Ω⋆ |Ω⋆ ⟩ + ⟨Ω⋆ |Γ† Γ|Ω⋆ ⟩ − 2⟨Ω⋆ |Γ|Ω⋆ ⟩ = 2 − 2⟨Ω⋆ |Γ|Ω⋆ ⟩ = 0.
Consequently, Γ|Ω⋆ ⟩ = |Ω⋆ ⟩. Since Γ is a permutation matrix, any basis vector with positive
amplitude cannot be sent outside the support of positive amplitudes. Thus, we obtain
                                  Γ(K) ⊆ K        and |Γ(K)| = |K|,                               (7.2)
where the second identity holds because Γ is bijective. Therefore, Equation (7.2) implies that
                                  Γ(K) = K         and Γ|K⟩ = |K⟩.                                (7.3)
Here, |K⟩ is the initialized full state of |ϕ⟩, corresponding to the set K, where the product subset
state |ϕ⟩ := |S1 ⟩⊗|S2 ⟩ corresponds to the set S1 ×S2 . Finally, using Equation (7.3), we complete
the proof by noting that ⟨Ωϕ |Γ|Ωϕ ⟩ = ⟨Ωϕ |Ωϕ ⟩ = 1, with |Ωϕ ⟩ := |ϕ⟩|0̄⟩|+̄⟩. as desired.

Testing rectangular closure. With this rectangular structure of non-negative product states
established in Lemma 7.4, we are now ready to understand how a reversible circuit, or equiva-
lently a permutation, acts on such states in combinatorial terms.
    To formalize this perspective, we need to introduce some additional notation. Specifically,
we refer to a Cartesian product A × B of two sets A, B ⊆ {0, 1}ℓ as a rectangle. As stated
in Lemma 3.13, without loss of generality, for any SepRCD instance (R0 , R1 ), it suffices to
consider the reversible circuit pair (Γ, I), which achieves the same parameters and thresholds.
Here, Γ can be viewed as a permutation matrix. We need the following notions:

   • Initialized sector: We denote the (m0 , r)-initialized sector over the rectangle S × T ,
     where S, T ⊆ {0, 1}ℓ and U := {0, 1}r , by
                                     K(S, T ) := S × T × {0m0 } × U.

   • Good and bad transitions: For any (a, b, u) ∈ A × B × U , where A, B ⊆ {0, 1}ℓ , we
     define the transition under Γ by
                                       Γ(a, b, 0m0 , u) = (a′ , b′ , z ′ , u′ ).
      If z ′ ̸= 0m0 , we call this a bad transition. Otherwise, we call it a good transition and write
                                              Γ0̄ (a, b, u) = (a′ , b′ ).

    Next, we define the notions of closed rectangle and rectangular closure. The former notion is
a static certificate: once the rectangle S × T is chosen, Definition 7.5 asks whether Γ maps the
initialized sector over that rectangle back into the same sector. By contrast, the latter notion
is a dynamic construction introduced in Definition 7.6: the underlying procedure starts with a


                                                     53
pair (a0 , b0 ), repeatedly updates the sets S + and T + for the current round, and either reaches
a closed rectangle or encounters a bad transition.

Definition 7.5 (Closed rectangle). For any given permutation Γ, a rectangle S × T ⊆ A × B is
closed with respect to Γ if the following inclusion holds.
                                          Γ(K(S, T )) ⊆ K(S, T )
Equivalently, this condition means that every transition that starts in the (m0 , r)-initialized sector
over the rectangle S × T stays in the initialized sector over the same rectangle.

Definition 7.6 (Rectangular closure from a starting pair). Fix a starting pair (a0 , b0 ) ∈ A × B.
Let S0 := {a0 } and T0 := {b0 }. For sets St and Tt that correspond to round t, we say that round
t is bad if there exist a ∈ St , b ∈ Tt , and u ∈ U such that
                          Γ(a, b, 0m0 , u) = (a′ , b′ , z ′ , u′ )   with   z ′ ̸= 0m0 .
If round t is not bad, we say that this round is good and define
                 St+1 := St ∪ a′ ∃a ∈ St , b ∈ Tt , u ∈ U, Γ0̄ (a, b, u) = (a′ , b′ ) ,
                             

                 Tt+1 := Tt ∪ b′ ∃a ∈ St , b ∈ Tt , u ∈ U, Γ0̄ (a, b, u) = (a′ , b′ ) .
                             


7.2    Rectangular closure testing and its consequences
We now formalize our intuition for testing rectangular closure and present the actual algorithm
in Algorithm 2. In particular, the procedure described in Definition 7.6 is implemented in Lines
5–16 of Algorithm 2.

 Algorithm 2: Rectangular Closure Testing Algorithm.
   Input         : A reversible circuit Γ.
   Output        : ACCEPT or REJECT.
   Parameters: The soundness parameter γ ∈ (0, 1), the witness length 2ℓ, the number of
                   |0⟩ ancillary qubits m0 , and the number of |+⟩ ancillary qubits r.
 1 Set L := ⌈(2ℓ ln 2 + 1)/ ln(1 + γ)⌉.
 2 Set A := {0, 1}ℓ , B := {0, 1}ℓ , and U := {0, 1}r .
 3 For each starting pair (a0 , b0 ) ∈ A × B :
 4    Initialize S0 := {a0 } and T0 := {b0 }.
      // Rectangular closure from (a0 , b0 ).
 5    For each round t ∈ {0, 1, · · · , L − 1} :
 6        Set temporary sets S + := St and T + := Tt .
 7        For every triple (a, b, u) ∈ St × Tt × U :
 8            Compute Γ(a, b, 0m0 , u) = (a′ , b′ , z ′ , u′ ).
 9            If z ′ ̸= 0m0 :
10                Declare that round t is bad .
11                Break.
12            Else:
13                Update S + ← S + ∪ {a′ } and T + ← T + ∪ {b′ }.
14        If round t is bad :
15            Break.
16        Set St+1 := S + and Tt+1 := T + .
17    If all rounds t ∈ {0, 1, · · · , L − 1} are good for (a0 , b0 ) :
18        Return ACCEPT.
19 Return REJECT.




                                                         54
   Next, we present the performance guarantees and detailed complexity bounds of Algorithm
2 under two different implementations, and the proof can be found in Section 7.3:
Theorem 7.7 (Explicit rectangular closure testing algorithms for SepRCD). For every effi-
ciently computable function γ(n) taking values in (0, 1), there is an L-round rectangular closure
testing algorithm for SepRCD1−ε,1−γ (ℓ, m0 , r), with the parameters
                                           
                           2ℓ ln 2 + 1       ℓ                       L+1
                     L=                  =O        and ε ≤ 25−r−2 (ℓ+4) .
                           ln(1 + γ)         γ
In particular, let TΓ and SΓ denote the time and workspace needed to evaluate Γ on the input
(a, b, 0m0 , u) ∈ A × B × {0m0 } × U , and let
                                 λ := 2ℓ + m0 + r + ⌈log(L + 2)⌉.
Then, Algorithm 2 can be implemented deterministically in either of the following ways:
 (1) The recursive implementation takes time (22ℓ+r )O(L) · poly(L, 2ℓ , 2r ) · TΓ and space O(Lλ +
     SΓ ).
 (2) The explicit-table implementation takes time O 24ℓ+r TΓ and space O(2ℓ + λ + SΓ ).
                                                              


7.2.1     Complexity-theoretic consequences of Theorem 7.7
Combined with Lemma 3.13, Theorem 7.7 implies the complexity-theoretic consequences stated
in Theorems 7.1 and 7.2, upon identifying the parameter r used below with the parameter m+
used in those theorems. Let gΓ (n) be the number of gates in Γ. Since all parameters ℓ(n), r(n),
m0 (n), and gΓ (n) are polynomially bounded, it holds that
      TΓ = O(gΓ · (2ℓ + m0 + r)) = poly(n) and SΓ = O(2ℓ + m0 + r + log gΓ ) = poly(n).
Consequently, we obtain the following bounds for StoqMA(k ) with nearly perfect completeness:
 (1) For the recursive implementationin Theorem 7.7(1), the underlying     algorithm runs in
     deterministic exp O γ max{ℓ, r} time and O γ max{ℓ, r, m0 } space. By combining
                           ℓ                          ℓ
                                                                       
                        e
     with prover compression (Corollary 4.2(2)),
                                                we obtain an kℓalgorithm for StoqMA(k ) that
     runs in deterministic exp O γ 2 max{kℓ, r} time and O γ 2 max{kℓ, r, m0 } space.
                                 kℓ
                                                                                 
                               e
        When γ(n) ≥ 1/ poly(n), the allowed completeness error ε(n) is inverse doubly exponential
        in n, and the space complexity is polynomial in n, yielding the PSPACE upper bound.
 (2) For the explicit-table implementation in Theorem 7.7(2), the underlying algorithm runs in
     deterministic O 24ℓ+r ·poly(n) = exp O(max{ℓ,    r}) time and O(2ℓ ) space. By combining
                                                         
                                              e
     with prover compression (Corollary 4.2(2)), we obtain an algorithm for StoqMA(k ) that
     runs in deterministic exp O(max{kℓ,
                                  e         r}) time and O(2kℓ ) space.
        When γ(n) ≥ 1/ exp(poly(n)), the allowed completeness error ε(n) is inverse triply expo-
        nential in n, and the deterministic runtime is exp O(max{kℓ,
                                                           e         r}) ≤ 2poly(n) , which leads
        to the EXP upper bound.

7.3     Analysis of Algorithm 2
We will analyze the correctness of Algorithm 2 separately for yes and no instances, namely the
completeness and soundness, and then put everything together.

Completeness. We begin with the perfect completeness case as a warm-up:
Lemma 7.8 (Perfectly agreeing pairs imply rectangular closure). Let (Γ, I) be a perfectly agree-
ing pair, viewed as a yes instance of SepRCD1,β (ℓ, m0 , r). Then, there exists a starting pair
whose rectangular closure never encounters a bad round.

                                                55
Proof. By utilizing Lemma 7.4, there exist non-empty sets S, T ⊆ {0, 1}ℓ such that Γ|K(S, T )⟩ =
|K(S, T )⟩, where the initialized sector K(S, T ) = S × T × {0m0 } × U and U = {0, 1}r . Since Γ
is a permutation, it follows that
                                           Γ(K(S, T )) = K(S, T )                               (7.4)
   Next, pick any starting pair (a0 , b0 ) ∈ S × T . We will prove by induction that
                              St ⊆ S and Tt ⊆ T hold for all rounds t,
and hence no round is bad. It is easy to see that the induction hypothesis holds at round t = 0,
because S0 = {a0 } and T0 = {b0 }.
   Now assume that the induction hypothesis holds at round t, equivalently, it holds that:
              If a ∈ St ⊆ S, b ∈ Tt ⊆ T, and u ∈ U,        then (a, b, 0m0 , u) ∈ K(S, T ).
    According to Equation (7.4), the image of Γ lies again in S × T × {0m0 } × U , and thus the
transition in this round is not bad. Therefore, any newly added coordinates (a′ , b′ ) satisfy a′ ∈ S
and b′ ∈ T , which establishes the induction hypothesis at round t + 1 via
                                      St+1 ⊆ S     and Tt+1 ⊆ T.

    Next, we relax the perfect completeness requirement in Lemma 7.8 and extend the result to
the setting in which the completeness error is sufficiently small relative to the number of rounds:

Lemma 7.9 (Near perfectly agreeing pairs imply rectangular closure in bounded rounds). Let
(Γ, I) be an ε-near perfectly agreeing pair, viewed as a yes instance of SepRCD1−ε,β (ℓ, m0 , r).
If the completeness error satisfies the upper bound
                         ε ≤ τL2 /2r+3 ,    where τ0 := 2−ℓ and τt+1 := τt2 /16,
then there exists a starting pair whose rectangular closure does not encounter a bad round up to
round L.

Proof. Let |ψ1 ⟩|ψ2 ⟩ be a non-negative product state whose initialized full state |Ω⟩ satisfies
                                             ⟨Ω|Γ|Ω⟩ ≥ 1 − ε.                                   (7.5)
Let p1 and p2 be probability distributions induced by the states |ψ1 ⟩ and |ψ2 ⟩, respectively,
defined by
                ∀x ∈ {0, 1}ℓ , p1 (x) := |⟨x|ψ1 ⟩|2 and p2 (x) := |⟨x|ψ2 ⟩|2 .
Since both distributions p1 and p2 are defined over [2ℓ ], there exist a0 and b0 such that
                            p1 (a0 ) ≥ 2−ℓ = τ0    and p2 (b0 ) ≥ 2−ℓ = τ0 .
We use this (a0 , b0 ) as the starting pair.

   We first bound the global near-invariance error. Since Γ is a permutation and ⟨Ω|Ω⟩ = 1,
together with Equation (7.5), we obtain
     ∥Γ|Ω⟩ − |Ω⟩∥22 = ⟨Ω|Γ† Γ|Ω⟩ + ⟨Ω|Ω⟩ − 2⟨Ω|Γ|Ω⟩ = 2 − 2⟨Ω|Γ|Ω⟩ ≤ 2 − 2(1 − ε) = 2ε.
Consequently, for every coordinate y ∈ {0, 1}2ℓ+m0 +r , it follows that
                                                         √

S0 = {a0 } and T0 = {b0 }.

Therefore, we establish the induction hypothesis by concluding that all newly inserted coordi-

Soundness. We now prove the soundness part:

Lemma 7.10 (Rectangle size growth for no instances before encountering a bad round). Let
(Γ, I) be a no instance of SepRCDα,1−γ (ℓ, m0 , r) for some γ > 0. Then, for every starting pair
and every round t, one of the following holds: either round t is bad, or the rectangle size grows
by a multiplicative factor of 1 + γ:
                                   |St+1 × Tt+1 | ≥ (1 + γ)|St × Tt |.                             (7.11)
Consequently, no starting pair can avoid encountering a bad round before round
                                                        
                                             2ℓ ln 2 + 1
                                      L :=                 .
                                             ln(1 + γ)
Proof. Fix a starting pair and a round t. Assume that round t is not bad. It therefore suffices
to prove that Equation (7.11). To do so, we show that the rectangle size grows exponentially
even when one restricts attention to product subset state witnesses.
    Consider a valid product subset witness state |St ⟩|Tt ⟩, whose initialized full state is uniform




                                                   57
on K(St , Tt ) = St × Tt × {0m0 } × U . A direct calculation shows that
                     ⟨K(St , Tt )|Γ|K(St , Tt )⟩                                                     (7.12a)
                          1
                   =               |{y ∈ K(St , Tt ) | Γy ∈ K(St , Tt )}|                            (7.12b)
                     |K(St , Tt )|
                          1
                   = r             |{(a, b, u) ∈ St × Tt × U | Γ0̄ (a, b, u) ∈ St × Tt }|.           (7.12c)
                     2 |St × Tt |
By combining the soundness promise ⟨K(St , Tt )|Γ|K(St , Tt )⟩ ≤ 1 − γ with Equation (7.12), we
obtain a lower bound on the number of triples escaping from St × Tt × U :
                                                                    / St × Tt }| ≥ γ2r |St × Tt |.
     Esc(St , Tt , U ) := |{(a, b, u) ∈ St × Tt × U | Γ0̄ (a, b, u) ∈                                 (7.13)
    Because Γ is injective, these leaving triples (a, b, u) have distinct outputs (a′ , b′ ) = Γ0̄ (a, b, u).
For each output pair (a′ , b′ ), there are at most |U | = 2r possible choices of output random
strings u′ . Consequently, the number of distinct new output pairs outside St × Tt is at least
Esc(St , Tt , U )/|U |. Since all these new output pairs are included in St+1 × Tt+1 , it follows that
                                                Esc(St , Tt , U )
                    |St+1 × Tt+1 | ≥ |St × Tt | +
