                                                γ2r |St × Tt |
                                 ≥ |St × Tt | +                 = (1 + γ)|St × Tt |.
                                                      2r
Here, the second line uses Equation (7.13), thereby establishing Equation (7.11).
   If a starting pair were to reach round L without encountering a bad round, then combining
Equation (7.11) with |S0 × T0 | = 1 would yield
                           |SL × TL | ≥ (1 + γ)L |S0 × T0 | = (1 + γ)L > 22ℓ .                        (7.14)
Here, the last inequality follows from the definition of L. Since SL × TL ⊆ {0, 1}ℓ × {0, 1}ℓ ,
Equation (7.14) leads to a contradiction. Therefore, no starting pair can reach round L without
encountering a bad round, as desired.

Putting everything together. Finally, we show the correctness of Algorithm 2:

Proof of Theorem 7.7. We first prove correctness separately for yes and no instances:

   • For yes instances, (Γ, I) is an ε-near perfectly agreeing pair, so there exists a non-negative
     product witness state whose initialized full state |Ω⟩ satisfies ⟨Ω|Γ|Ω⟩ ≥ 1 − ε. By
     Lemma 7.9, the error bound ε ≤ τL2 /2r+3 implies that the rectangular closure starting
     from some starting pair reaches round L without encountering a bad round. Therefore,
     Algorithm 2 accepts in this case.

   • For no instances, because (Γ, I) is a no instance, it follows that ⟨Ωψ |Γ|Ωψ ⟩ ≤ 1 − γ for all
     non-negative product witness states |ψ⟩. Using Lemma 7.10, no starting pair can reach
     round L without encountering a bad round. Hence, Algorithm 2 rejects.

    Next, we analyze two deterministic implementations of Algorithm 2:
Recursive implementation. For every fixed starting pair (a0 , b0 ), define indicator predicates
               ∀t ∈ {0, 1, · · · , L},    χSt (a) := I[a ∈ St ] and χTt (b) := I[b ∈ Tt ].
Initially, it holds that χS0 (a) = I[a = a0 ] and χT0 (b) = I[b = b0 ].
    For each round t < L, we first test whether round t is bad by evaluating the predicate
            Badt := ∃a, b, u I[χSt (a) ∧ χTt (b) ∧ Γ(a, b, 0m0 , u) ∈
                                                                    / A × B × {0m0 } × U ].




                                                     58
    If Badt is false, then the next-round predicates are simultaneously given by
           χSt+1 (a′ ) = χSt (a′ ) ∨ ∃a, b, b′ , u I χSt (a) ∧ χTt (b) ∧ Γ0̄ (a, b, u) = (a′ , b′ ) ,
                                                                                                   

            χTt+1 (b′ ) = χTt (b′ ) ∨ ∃a, a′ , b, u I χSt (a) ∧ χTt (b) ∧ Γ0̄ (a, b, u) = (a′ , b′ ) .
                                                                                                   

   A depth-first deterministic evaluation stores only one recursion path of length at most L,
together with a constant number of strings of total length O(λ) per level. Hence, the space is
O(Lλ + SΓ ). There are 22ℓ starting pairs, and at each level the deterministic search enumerates
at most 22ℓ+r triples. Thus, the running time is bounded by
                                      (22ℓ+r )O(L) · poly(L, 2ℓ , 2r ) · TΓ .
Explicit-table implementation. For each starting pair, Algorithm 2 stores St and Tt , together
with the newly added sets ∆St := St \ St−1 and ∆Tt := Tt \ Tt−1 , as bit tables of length 2ℓ ,
with the convention S−1 = T−1 = ∅. This implementation is still deterministic. During round t,
instead of scanning all triples in St × Tt × U , it suffices to scan, without duplication, only those
triples in                                                     
                                   (∆St × Tt ) ∪ (St × ∆Tt ) × U.
Indeed, every pair outside (∆St × Tt ) ∪ (St × ∆Tt ) already belonged to St−1 × Tt−1 , and hence
had already been scanned in an earlier round. If that earlier scan had found a bad transition,
the algorithm would already have rejected; otherwise, the corresponding output coordinates had
already been inserted into the current tables, so rescanning such a pair cannot create any new
element. Hence, for a fixed starting pair, each active pair in A × B is scanned at most once.
    For one starting pair, there are at most 22ℓ active pairs, and scanning one active pair requires
enumerating all u ∈ U and evaluating Γ, which costs O(2r TΓ ). Consequently, one starting pair
takes time O(22ℓ+r TΓ ). Since there are |A×B| = 22ℓ starting pairs, the total time is O(24ℓ+r TΓ ).
The space consists of a constant number of bit tables for St , Tt , ∆St , and ∆Tt , the current basis
strings and counters, and the workspace for evaluating Γ, namely O(2ℓ + λ + SΓ ).

    Finally, by the inequality γ/2 ≤ ln(1 + γ) ≤ γ for γ ∈ (0, 1), we simplify the round bound to
                                                         
                                         2ℓ ln 2 + 1        ℓ
                                   L=                  =O       .
                                         ln(1 + γ)          γ

8    A deterministic exponential-time upper bound for StoqMA(k )
In this section, we record an upper bound for StoqMA(k ), which follows from the Sum-of-Squares
algorithm of Barak–Kelner–Steurer [BKS14].

Theorem 8.1 ([BKS14, Theorem 3.1] with improved t-dependence). For any entrywise
non-negative, real-symmetric matrix M acting on (Cd )⊗t with ∥M ∥ ≤ 1, there is a degree-
O(t2 log d/ε2 ) Sum-of-Squares algorithm that approximates
                                              max        Tr M (xxT )⊗t
                                           x∈Rd :∥x∥=1

to within additive error ε.

    For completeness, we describe the BKS algorithm in the appendix. In addition, we slightly
sharpen BKS’s analysis, improving the dependence on the tensor order t from O(t3 log d/ε2 ), as
stated in [BKS14, Theorem 3.1], to the optimal dependence O(t2 log d/ε2 ) under ETH.25
    Since the matrix M arising from a stoquastic verification circuit is entrywise non-negative
and symmetric, we have the following upper bound:
  25
     The symmetry of the tensors is not important for this algorithm. The same algorithm works for asymmetric
tensors. But for our analysis to obtain O(t2 ) dependence, symmetry is crucial.



                                                         59
Theorem 8.2 (StoqMA(k ) ⊆ EXP). For all efficiently computable functions c(n) and ∆(n)
with 1/2 < c(n) ≤ 1 and c(n) − ∆(n) ≥ 1/2, and for every efficiently computable positive
integer-valued function k(n) and ℓ(n), the following inclusions hold:
                                                       h      k 2 ℓ2 i
                       SymStoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K ⊆ DTIME exp O                  ;             (8.1)
                                                                 ∆2
                                                       h      k 2 ℓ2 i
                           StoqMAℓ Jk, c, ∆K ⊆ DTIME exp O    e            .             (8.2)
                                                                 ∆2
In particular, for every efficiently computable integer-valued function k that is polynomially
bounded in n, the following inclusion holds:
                                       StoqMA(k ) ⊆ EXP.

    Here, Equation (8.1) is an application of Theorem 8.1 with t = k, d = 2ℓ , and ε = ∆/3,
which gives rise to a exp(O(k 2 ℓ2 /∆2 ))-size SDP; Equation (8.2) is a straightforward corollary
of Equation (8.1) and Theorem 4.11. Notably, the deterministic time bounds in Equations (8.1)
and (8.2) have an implicit exponential dependence on the number of |+⟩ ancillary qubits, arising
from transforming a stoquastic verification circuit Vx into the matrix MΓ :
Remark 8.3 (Implicit exponential dependence on the number of |+⟩ ancillary qubits). Let m0
and m+ be the numbers of |0⟩ and |+⟩ ancillary qubits. After contracting the initialized ancillary
registers, the Sum-of-Squares algorithm in Theorem 8.1 is applied to the matrix
            MΓ = ⟨0|⊗m0 ⟨+|⊗m+ Γ|0⟩⊗m0 |+⟩⊗m+ ∈ D+ (2kℓ ),       where Γ := Vx† XO Vx .
Consequently, the resulting tensor order and local dimension are k and 2ℓ , respectively, so the
degree and size of the resulting SDP have no explicit dependence on m+ . Nevertheless, in the
succinct-circuit representation, the coefficients of MΓ satisfy
           ⟨b|MΓ |a⟩ = 2−m+ |{u ∈ {0, 1}m+ | Γ(a, 0m0 , u) ∈ {b} × {0m0 } × {0, 1}m+ }|.
Therefore, the m+ -independence applies to the SDP-size bound once MΓ is available, whereas
deterministically constructing its coefficients directly from Vx incurs a 2O(m+ ) preprocessing cost
unless an efficient coefficient-access procedure is assumed.

ETH-based optimality. A remarkable consequence is the essential optimality under ETH of
the above upper bound result, and both lower bounds Theorems 5.1 and 5.4 obtained in Section 5.
Since Dinur’s PCP (Theorem 5.8) can be reduced from SAT instances in near-linear time, the
two StoqMA protocols for Dinur’s PCP (Theorems 5.1 and 5.4) together with Equations (8.1)
and (8.2) both imply a exp(O(n))
                            e     algorithm for SAT.

Corollary 8.4 (ETH-based optimality of Theorems 5.4, 8.1 and 8.2). Under ETH, up to lower-
order factors, the following statements hold:

 (1) Equation (8.1) in Theorem 8.2 is tight with respect to the number of provers k in view
     of Theorem 5.1; consequently, the BKS algorithm in Theorem 8.1 is tight with respect to
     the order of tensor t.

 (2) Equation (8.1) in Theorem 8.2 is tight with respect to the proof length ℓ in view of Theo-
     rem 5.4; consequently, the BKS algorithm in Theorem 8.1 is tight with respect to the log
     dimension log d.

The same holds for Equation (8.2).

A    PSPACE ⊆ PreciseStoqMA1
In this section, we establish the equivalence between PreciseStoqMA1 , PreciseStoqMA, and
PSPACE, where PreciseStoqMA1 denotes PreciseStoqMA with perfect completeness:

Theorem A.1 (PreciseStoqMA with perfect completeness contains PSPACE). There exists an
explicit efficiently computable function s⋆ (n) = 1 − 2− poly(n) such that
                               PSPACE ⊆ PreciseStoqMA(1, s⋆ ).

    Then, combining Theorem A.1 with PreciseQMA ⊆ PSPACE [FL16, FL18] immediately yields
the following equivalence, thereby giving a positive answer to and fully resolving the open prob-
lem in [Liu21, Section 1.2] asking whether PreciseStoqMA captures the full PSPACE power:

Corollary A.2. PreciseStoqMA1 = PreciseStoqMA = PSPACE.


   To establish Theorem A.1, we begin with the PSPACE-complete problem Clean Con-
nected Component, introduced in [AG21]:

                                               70
Definition A.3 (Clean Connected Component, CleanCC, adapted from [AG21, Definition
3.1]). Let G = (V, E) be a graph with vertex set V = {0, 1}n , and suppose that every vertex
v ∈ V has degree d(v) ≤ dG ≤ poly(n). We say that a pair of polynomial-size classical circuits
(CG , CM ), where CG : V × JdG K → V and CM : V → {0, 1}, is an instance of CleanCC(n, dG )
if the following conditions hold for every v ∈ V :

 (1) For each j ∈ JdG K, CG (v, j) outputs the j-th neighbor of v. Furthermore, every neighbor
     of v appears exactly once in the list CG (v, 0), · · · , CG (v, dG − 1), and every remaining slot
     is padded with the self-loop value v.

 (2) CM (v) outputs 1 if the vertex v is marked; otherwise, it outputs 0, and v is unmarked.

The problem is to decide which of the following holds:

   • Yes. There exists a non-empty connected component H⋆ ⊆ V such that all vertices in H⋆
     are unmarked.

   • No. Every connected component H ⊆ V contains at least one marked vertex.

Lemma A.4 (Adapted from [AG21, Appendix D]). CleanCC is PSPACE-complete.

   Now we are ready to state the main technical result in this section, which, together with
Lemma A.4, establishes Theorem A.1:

Theorem A.5 (CleanCC ∈ PreciseStoqMA1 ). For any efficiently computable function dG (n)
bounded by a polynomial in n, the following holds:
                                                                
                                                         1
                   CleanCC(n, dG ) ∈ StoqMA 1, 1 − 2n+2           .
                                                   2    (dG + 1)

A.1    Constructing the stoquastic verification circuit
We first make each move from a vertex u ∈ V to its j-th neighbor CG (u, j) reversible by
introducing the return-index circuit C
                                     eG , whose efficient construction is given below:

Proposition A.6 (Return-index circuit can be efficiently constructed). Given the circuit CG
that describes the graph G = (V, E) with |V | = 2n and the maximum degree dG ≤ poly(n), one
                                              eG : V × JdG K → JdG K satisfying the following:
can efficiently construct a classical circuit C

   • For every edge (u, v) ∈ E and every neighbor index j with CG (u, j) = v, define C
                                                                                     eG (u, j) to
     be the unique return index at v, namely the unique index satisfying CG v, CG (u, j) = u.
                                                                                         
                                                                                 e

   • For every padded self-loop port with CG (u, j) = u, define C
                                                                eG (u, j) = j.

Proof. For every edge (u, v) ∈ E such that CG (u, j) = v, C
                                                          eG (u, j) can be implemented efficiently
by simply checking all j ∈ JdG K and finding the unique index j⋆′ satisfying CG (v, j⋆′ ) = u. This
                          ′

construction is efficient because the maximum degree dG ≤ poly(n).

   Next, we introduce the classical reversible circuit ΓG , built from the classical circuits CG and
eG and made reversible by using additional |0⟩ ancillary qubits, as defined by

   With these reversible circuits in place, we present the stoquastic verification circuit for
CleanCC used in the proof of Theorem A.5, as given in Protocol 7.

  Protocol 7: A stoquastic verification circuit VCG ,CM for CleanCC.
   Input       : A CleanCC(n, dG ) instance (CG , CM ).
   Parameters: q := ⌈log(dG + 1)⌉ and J := 2q .
   Registers : B: the q-qubit branch-index register.
                  V: the n-qubit vertex-label register.
                  M: the single-qubit marking register.
                  A: the register containing |0⟩ ancillary qubits.
   1. Construct the classical reversible circuits ΓG and ΓM , as expressed in
    Equations (A.1) and (A.2), using Proposition A.6.

       2. Define a classical reversible circuit Γ on the quantum registers (B, V, M, A) as follows:
        (1) Apply ΓG to the registers (B, V, A) when 0 ≤ j < dG ;
        (2) Apply ΓM to the registers (V, M, A) when j = dG ;
        (3) Do nothing otherwise.
       Equivalently, Γ can be expressed as

    We now proceed with the proof of Theorem A.5. At a high level, for yes instances, the
subset state |H⋆ ⟩ serves as the witness state. For no instances, however, the analysis is more
delicate, since it must apply to all non-negative witness states. The key observation is that,
in any connected component containing a marked vertex, large witness mass must create either
noticeable mass on a marked vertex or noticeable amplitude differences across edges. This
argument is a path form of the Poincaré principle (cf. [Jer03, Section 5.2]): local edge differences,
together with marked-vertex mass, control the total mass in the component.

Proof of Theorem A.5. We begin by expanding ⟨Ωψ |Γ|Ωψ ⟩ according to the

B     The Barak-Kelner-Steurer algorithm and a refined analysis
We refer the reader to the original paper of Barak, Kelner, and Steurer [BKS14, Section 1] for
the background on the sum-of-squares algorithm. Our goal is to prove the following theorem for
the purpose of completeness:

Theorem 8.1 ([BKS14, Theorem 3.1] with improved t-dependence, restated). For any entrywise
non-negative, real-symmetric matrix M acting on (Cd )⊗t with ∥M ∥ ≤ 1, there is a degree-
O(t2 log d/ε2 ) Sum-of-Squares algorithm that approximates
                                            max        Tr M (xxT )⊗t
                                         x∈Rd :∥x∥=1

to within additive error ε.

    Our treatment largely follows Barak–Kelner–Steurer’s proof, with similar notation. We use
“degree” and “level” interchangeably: a degree-D pseudodistribution (resp., pseudoexpectation)
is what they would call a level-D pseudodistribution (resp., pseudoexpectation). We improve
the analysis to have the optimal dependence on t, the order of the tensor, but otherwise the
same. The main tool underlying their algorithm is the “reweighting”:


                                                       74
Definition B.1 (Conditioning, or reweighting). Let X be a random variable over the (d − 1)-

dimensional sphere, and E
polynomial with Ex∼X [B] > 0. The reweighted pseudoexpectation for X | B is defined by

                                                              E
for every polynomial P of degree at most D − deg(B).

Lemma B.2 (Direct rounding). Let X be a level-2t pseudodistribution satisfying (B.1)-(B.2).
If                                                           
                         dH {A1 · · · At }, {A1 } · · · {At } ≤ ε,

Reweighting. If direct rounding fails to provide a good approximation, then
                                                               
                           dH {A1 · · · At }, {A1 } · · · {At } ≳ ε.
Hence the variables Ai are somewhat correlated, and one can pin some variables to make the
remaining variables more deterministic. Updating the pseudodistribution by reweighting (con-
ditioning on the pinnings) iteratively decreases the entropy of the variables Ai . Since entropy
is bounded, within a bounded number of iterations, the Hellinger distance must be small and
at that time the direct rounding would work. This is the part where we tighten the analysis in
BKS exploiting the chain rule and symmetry bypassing a wasteful triangle inequality.




                                                            76
Lemma B.3 (Entropy decrement per conditioning round). If
                                                             
                         dH {A1 · · · At }, {A1 } · · · {At } > ε,
then

                                       KL(P ∥Q) ≥ 2 dH (P, Q)2 > 2ε2 .                                            (B.5)

C     Omitted proofs
C.1    Omitted proofs in Section 3
We first show the multiplicativity of stoquastic separable values of product Hermitian matrices:

Lemma 3.9 (Stoquastic separable values of product Hermitian matrices are multiplicative, re-
stated). Let M
Q

                Q and ′M be entrywise non-negative product Hermitian matrices of dimensions
   i∈[k] di and  i∈[k] di , respectively, and suppose that these matrices admit the product decompo-

                              M = M1 ⊗· · ·⊗Mk and M ′ = M1′ ⊗· · ·⊗Mk′ ,                       (3.7)
where each factor Mi and Mi′ is Hermitian and entrywise non-negative for i ∈ [k]. Then the
stoquastic separable value is multiplicative under tensor products, namely
              hSep+ (d1 d′ ,··· ,dk d′ ) M ⊗M ′ = hSep+ (d1 ,··· ,dk ) (M ) · hSep+ (d′ ,··· ,d′ ) (M ′ ).

                         1           k                                                1        k


Proof. For simplicity, we prove only the case where k = 2, and the same argument directly
extends to general k. Similar to Proposition 3.7, it suffices to consider only non-negative quantum
states. Also, the lower bound is straightforward, following the same argument as that used for
the corresponding part of the proof of Lemma 3.8.
    We now establish the upper bound. Let the operator M act on quantum registers A and B,
and let the operator M ′ act on quantum registers A′ and B′ . After regrouping the registers as
AA′ and BB′ , we obtain
                          MAB ⊗ MA′ ′ B′ = (M1 ⊗ M1′ )AA′ ⊗ (M2 ⊗ M2′ )BB′

from Equation (3.7). Then it follows that:

Here, the fourth line follows from Theorem 3 in [Gan00, Section VIII.3], which states that the
absolute value of each eigenvalue of an entrywise non-negative matrix A is at most the largest
eigenvalue λmax (A). The fifth line uses the fact that λmax (A) = max|ψ⟩ ⟨ψ|A|ψ⟩ = ⟨ψ⋆ |A|ψ⋆ ⟩ for
some non-negative unit vector |ψ⋆ ⟩ that attains the optimum.

   Similar to the notion of separable in [HM13], one may wonder whether Lemma 3.9 extends to
convex combinations of such matrices, which we refer to as product-convex Hermitian matrices.
An explicit counterexample can be found in an earlier version of our work [LW26, Remark 3.10].

C.2    Omitted proof of the generalized birthday paradox
Lemma 5.12 (A generalized birthday paradox, restated). Let Ω be a finite set of size n, µ be a
probability distribution on Ω, and B ⊆ Ω × Ω be a symmetric relation representing the “birthday
collisions.” For some subset Ω0 ⊆ Ω, if for some parameters α, β > 0 and some integer D > 0,
the following are true

                                                               79
 (1) µ(x) ≤ α/n for every x ∈ Ω0 ;
 (2) for every x ∈ Ω0 , the number of y ∈ Ω0 with (x, y) ∈ B is at most D;
 (3) the bad-pair weight on Ω0 satisfies
                                                X                          β
                                   λ :=                       µ(x)µ(y) ≥     .
                                                                           n
                                          (x,y)∈B∩(Ω0 ×Ω0 )

Then for any constant ε > 0, there is a large enough C such that the following holds for all
                                                                             √
sufficiently large n: Let X1 , . . . , XK be i.i.d. samples from µ with K = C n,
                        h                                              i
                      Pr ∃i < j such that (Xi , Xj ) ∈ B ∩ (Ω0 × Ω0 ) ≥ 1 − ε.

Proof. Let Iij be the indicator that (Xi , Xj ) ∈ B ∩ (Ω0 × Ω0 ), and
                                                 X
                                       Z :=            Iij .
                                                 1≤i<j≤K
                                               √
               K
Then E[Z] = 2 λ. Because λ ≥ β/n and K = C n, we have E[Z] ≥ βC 2 /3 for all sufficiently
                 

large n.
    We claim that E[Z 2 ] = O(1) + O(E[Z]2 ), with constants depending only on α, β, D, C.
Expand                             X            X
                              Z2 =    Iij + 2         Iij Ikℓ .                      (C.2)
                                          i<j         (i,j)<(k,ℓ)

There are three kinds of terms in the above equation:
Diagonal terms. These contribute exactly E[Z].
Disjoint pairs of pairs. If {i, j} ∩ {k, ℓ} = ∅, then Iij and Ikℓ are independent, so
                                            E[Iij Ikℓ ] = λ2 .
The total contribution of all disjoint pairs of pairs is therefore O(K 4 λ2 ), which is O(E[Z]2 ).
Overlapping pairs of pairs. It remains to bound terms such as E[Iij Iik ]. Define
                                            X             Dα
                                 s(x) :=           µ(y) ≤     ,
                                                           n
                                           y∈Ω0 :(x,y)∈B

where the inequality is due to our assumption (i) and (ii). Hence
                                  X             Dα X                 Dα
                    E[Iij Iik ] =   µ(x)s(x)2 ≤           µ(x)s(x) =    λ.
                                                  n                   n
                                x∈Ω0                       x∈Ω0

Because λ ≤ Dα/n as well, this contribution is O(1/n2 ). There are only O(K 3 ) = O(n3/2 )
overlapping pairs of pairs, so their total contribution is O(K 3 /n2 ) = o(1).
   Combining all contributions,
                                 E[Z 2 ] ≤ E[Z] + O(E[Z]2 ) + o(1).
Choose C large enough so that E[Z] ≥ 1 for all sufficiently large n. Then, for large n,
                                           E[Z 2 ] ≤ A E[Z]2
for some constant A = A(α, β, D, C) > 0. Paley–Zygmund now yields
                                                     E[Z]2  1
                                       Pr[Z > 0] ≥      2
                                                           ≥ .
                                                     E[Z ]  A
    To further amplify the above probability to 1 − ε, we increase the number of samples by a
factor of log(1/ε). More precisely, we repeat the experiment in O(log(1/ε)) independent blocks


                                                   80
and accept if any block finds a bad pair; this procedure reduces the failure probability to at
most ε. For constant ε, this procedure only changes the absolute constant C. This gives the
desired conclusion.

C.3    Omitted proofs of rETH-optimality of the BPTIME upper bound for
       StoqMA
Lemma 6.4 (Proof-length optimality under randomized ETH, restated). Let ℓ(n) be an effi-
ciently computable positive integer-valued function satisfying ℓ(n) ≤ poly(n). Assume that
                                                                       
                       StoqMAℓ J1, 1, 1/2K ⊆ BPTIME 2o(ℓ) · poly(|V |) ,

where |V | denotes the description size of the underlying stoquastic verification circuit V . Then
this randomized upper bound refutes randomized ETH.

Proof. Let φ be an n-variable SAT instance. We now provide an efficient construction of a
single-prover stoquastic verification circuit with witness length ℓ(n) = n, completeness 1, and
soundness 1/2. The witness register W consists of n qubits and is interpreted as an assignment
a ∈ {0, 1}n . Let Q be a single-qubit register initialized to |+⟩, let F be a single-qubit flag register
initialized to |0⟩, and let A be a poly(n)-qubit work register initialized to |0̄⟩ that includes a
single-qubit register B.
    The stoquastic verification circuit Vφ does the following:

 (1) Compute the bit SATφ (a) = I[φ(a) is satisfied] and write the resulting bit into B.

 (2) Apply XB ToffoliQ,B→F XB , which flips F when Q contains |1⟩ and φ(a) is unsatisfied.

 (3) Uncompute the work register and measure Q in the Hadamard basis.

Correctness analysis. For a basis witness |a⟩, if φ(a) is satisfied, then Vφ accepts with probability
                                                                                             √
1. If φ(a) is unsatisfied, then the resulting state in the registers (Q, F) is (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/ 2, and
thus Vφ accepts with probability 1/2.
    Now let us move on to the general case. Consider the witness state |ψ⟩ = a∈{0,1}n αa |a⟩,
                                                                                     P

where a |αa |2 = 1. By linearity, the acceptance probability can be expressed as
       P

                                            1 1              X
                      Pr[Vφ accepts |ψ⟩] = +                               |αa |2 .
                                            2 2            n
                                                    a∈{0,1} ∧ φ(a) is satisfied

    Consequently, if φ is satisfiable, a satisfying assignment basis state makes the verifier accept
with probability 1; whereas if φ is unsatisfiable, then every witness is accepted with proba-
bility exactly 1/2. Therefore, φ is efficiently mapped to a stoquastic verification circuit for
StoqMAℓ J1, 1, 1/2K with ℓ(n) = n and |Vφ | = poly(n). Together with the hypothesis, this reduc-
tion yields a randomized 2o(n) poly(n)-time algorithm for SAT, which contradicts randomized
ETH [DHM+ 14, Section 1.3] (see also [CRTY23, Hypothesis 1]).
